4.6
Event Activity and Centrality Definition

Observables are measured in narrow bins of variables reflecting event activity. Centrality, a measure of the collision’s geometric overlap, is determined using these activity variables.

4.6.1
Event Activity Variables

Key event activity estimators include:

•

N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
: The number of reconstructed tracks fulfilling specific quality criteria (typically HILOOSE with
0.5<p_{\text{T}}<5.0
GeV and
|\eta|<2.5
). Calculated as
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}=\sum_{i}1
.

•

N_{\mathrm{ch}}
: The efficiency- and fake-corrected
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
. Calculated as
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=\sum_{i}w_{i}
, where
w_{i}
is the track correction weight (defined in Eq.
4.4
).

•

FCal
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
: The transverse energy deposited in the Forward Calorimeter.

Observables are initially measured in fine bins (e.g., unit-width in
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
/
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
, or 1 GeV in FCal
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
) and then rebinned.

4.6.1.1
Distributions and Correlations in Pb+Pb Collisions

Figure
4.16
shows the distributions of FCal
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
,
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
, and their correlation in Pb+Pb collisions.

Figure 4.16
:
Distributions of FCal–
\sum E_{T}
(left),
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
(middle), and the correlation between
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
and FCal–
\sum E_{T}
(right) in Pb+Pb collisions.

4.6.1.2
Distributions and Correlations in Xe+Xe Collisions

Figure
4.17
displays the FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
distribution (left),
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
distribution (middle), and their correlation (right) for Xe+Xe collisions.

Figure 4.17
:
(Left) Distribution of FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
in Xe+Xe collisions. (Middle) Distribution of
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
in Xe+Xe collisions. (Right) Correlation between FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
and
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
in Xe+Xe collisions.

4.6.2
Centrality Definition

Centrality classes are defined by fitting the FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
distribution with a Glauber model. The use of FCal
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
is motivated by the “two-component model” of particle production, where soft particle production (transverse energy at large
\eta
) scales with a linear combination of
N_{\mathrm{part}}
(number of participant nucleons) and
N_{\mathrm{coll}}
(number of binary nucleon-nucleon collisions)
[
117
,
118
]
.

4.6.2.1
Centrality Thresholds

The FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
thresholds for centrality definitions in Pb+Pb collisions are listed in Appendix Table
6.1

[
119
]
. Centrality classes for Xe+Xe collisions are analogously defined based on FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
thresholds from a Glauber model fit. These thresholds, are listed in Appendix in Table
6.2
.

The centrality distributions in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe are shown in Fig.
4.18
. The distribution is flat in the case of Xe+Xe and Pb+Pb except in peripheral Pb+Pb in 70-80
\%
centrality where there are some deviations.

Figure 4.18
:
centrality distributions in Pb+Pb collisions (left) and in Xe+Xe collisions (right). The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

4.7
Mitigation of Detector Effects

Additional corrections are applied to mitigate residual detector-induced effects, relevant for both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collision analyses.

4.7.1
Track Azimuthal Distribution Flattening

In heavy-ion collisions, the particle azimuthal angle (
\phi
) distribution, averaged over many events, is expected to be uniform due to random event plane orientation. Observed non-uniformities indicate detector-induced artifacts. While MC-derived tracking efficiency corrections handle
\eta
and
p_{\text{T}}
dependencies, residual
\phi
dependent variations, potentially arising from detector imperfections or acceptance, require an additional “flattening” correction.

A correction factor
w_{\phi}
is defined as:

w_{\phi}(\mathrm{cent},z_{vtx},\mathrm{charge},p_{\text{T}},\eta,\phi)=\frac{\langle N(\delta\eta)\rangle_{\phi}}{N(\delta\eta,\delta\phi)}

(4.5)

where
N(\delta\eta,\delta\phi)
is the particle count in a differential
(\delta\eta,\delta\phi)
window, and
\langle N(\delta\eta)\rangle_{\phi}
is the average particle count in that
\delta\eta
window across all
\phi
. These
w_{\phi}
factors are calculated for both collision systems studied. Applying
w_{\phi}
ensures a uniform
\phi
distribution for narrow
\eta
intervals.
Since detector effects can depend on occupancy,
w_{\phi}
is evaluated for different centrality ranges and also depends on
z_{vtx}
, particle charge,
p_{\text{T}}
, and
\eta
.

4.7.2
Recentering of Flow Vectors

In flow analysis, “recentering” corrects residual detector non-uniformities affecting unit-flow vectors derived from the inner detector. This involves subtracting the run-averaged X and Y components of the event-wise q-vectors for each harmonic.

The recentered q-vector components,
q_{n,x}
and
q_{n,y}
, are:

\displaystyle q_{n,x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}\cos(n\phi_{i})}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}-\langle q_{n,x}\rangle

(4.6)

\displaystyle q_{n,y}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}\sin(n\phi_{i})}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}-\langle q_{n,y}\rangle

(4.7)

where
w_{i}
are particle weights (typically
w_{id}
),
\phi_{i}
are azimuthal angles,
n
is the harmonic number, and
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes the average over events within a specific data acquisition run. The q-vector is normalized by the sum of weights.

The recentering offsets,
\langle q_{n,x}\rangle
and
\langle q_{n,y}\rangle
, are determined in bins of
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
and for various
\eta
and
p_{\text{T}}
bins. These offsets generally show independence from event centrality, suggesting a detector azimuthal acceptance that does not vary significantly with event activity. Their magnitudes are typically small (
<0.001-0.005
) for all harmonics, much smaller than the expected true flow signal. Further details and plots for recentering corrections for both collision systems are provided in Appendix
6.9
.

The final weight for a particle reconstructed in the inner detector,
w_{i}
, combines the flattening correction
w_{\phi}
with the tracking efficiency
\epsilon
and fake rate
f
(from Section
4.5.3
):

w_{i}(\mathrm{cent},z_{vtx},\mathrm{charge},p_{\text{T}},\eta,\phi)=w_{\phi}(\mathrm{cent},z_{vtx},\mathrm{charge},p_{\text{T}},\eta,\phi)\frac{1-f(\mathrm{cent},p_{\text{T}},\eta)}{\epsilon(\mathrm{cent},p_{\text{T}},\eta)}

(4.8)

This weight
w_{i}
is used in all observables constructed from inner detector particles. Further details and plots for flattening weights for both collision systems are provided in Appendix
6.9
.

With the event-selection criteria and detector corrections now established, we proceed in the next chapter to measure the novel observables in heavy-ion collision systems.

Chapter 5
Measurements and Results

5.1
Evidence for Collective Radial Expansion of QGP

Despite theoretical models suggesting a collective nature for the radial expansion of the QGP in HIC, direct experimental evidence has remained elusive. This chapter provides the first comprehensive experimental evidence showing that the event-by-event fluctuations in radial expansion of QGP arises as a collective response of the medium.

The collective expansion of QGP manifests itself in two complementary forms: anisotropic and radial flow. Anisotropic flow arises from the initial geometric anisotropy present in the nuclear overlap region and are quantified by the coefficients
v_{n}
from the Fourier expansion of the azimuthal particle distribution. In contrast, radial flow describes the collective, isotropic outward expansion of the system. Extensive experimental measurements of
p_{\text{T}}
-differential studies of
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
and their event-by-event fluctuations
[
120
,
21
,
26
,
121
,
122
,
123
,
124
,
125
]
, have provided stringent constraints on both the initial conditions of the collision and the transport properties of the QGP medium.

The hydrodynamic response to fluctuations in the initial overlap region’s overall size leads to variations in radial flow. This phenomenon manifests as event-by-event fluctuations in the average transverse momentum,
\langle p_{T}\rangle
. Specifically, as shown in Figure
5.1
, events with similar total energy but a smaller initial transverse size are expected to have larger pressure gradients in the initial state, resulting in stronger radial expansion and
\langle p_{T}\rangle

[
126
,
127
]
.

Figure 5.1
:
Illustration of the relation between
R
(system size) and
\langle p_{T}\rangle
. For two systems with the same total entropy,
S
, the one with a smaller overlap area will experience larger pressure gradients, leading to a larger outward expansion or
\langle p_{T}\rangle

[
19
]
.

While anisotropic flow measurements primarily constrain the shear viscosity to entropy density ratio (
\eta/s
), theoretical model calculations indicate that fluctuations in radial flow are especially sensitive to the medium’s bulk viscosity to entropy density ratio (
\zeta/s
). This heightened sensitivity makes measurements of radial flow fluctuations a powerful complementary probe to anisotropic flow studies for characterizing the transport properties of the QGP. As illustrated in Figure
5.2
, IP-Glasma+MUSIC+UrQMD simulations that exclude bulk viscosity can reproduce the centrality dependence of anisotropic flow coefficients like
v_{n}\{2\}
but overestimate the event-averaged mean transverse momentum,
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
. Conversely, the inclusion of bulk viscosity in these models effectively suppresses
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, leading to improved agreement with experimental data.

Figure 5.2
:
(Left) Ensemble-averaged event-wise transverse momentum,
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, and (Right) anisotropic flow coefficients
v_{n}\{2\}
as functions of centrality. Bands around the dashed lines indicate the effect of varying the switching temperature for the start of hydrodynamic expansion
T_{\rm switch}
in model calculations
[
20
]
. Data points are from the ALICE Collaboration
[
21
,
22
]
.

5.1.1
Theoretical Background

5.1.1.1
Defining the
p_{\text{T}}
-differential Radial Flow Observable

Variations in the initial overlap-region size and energy density of a heavy-ion collision leave a distinct imprint on the final-state event-wise average transverse momentum, denoted as
[p_{\text{T}}]
. This is evident from hydrodynamic simulations shown in Figure
5.3
, demonstrating strong correlations between
[p_{\text{T}}]
and both the initial transverse size
R
and the initial energy per unit rapidity
E_{i}
.

Figure 5.3
:
Ideal hydrodynamic results for Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5.02\ \mathrm{TeV}
and impact parameter
b=2.5\ \mathrm{fm}
. Left: Event-wise average transverse momentum
[p_{\text{T}}]
vs. initial size
R
. Right:
[p_{\text{T}}]
vs. initial energy per unit rapidity
E_{i}
. Figure adapted from Ref.
[
23
]
.

To quantify these correlations and to test whether fluctuations in radial flow indeed arise as a consequence of initial-state variations, Ref.
[
24
]
correlated
[p_{\text{T}}]
with several initial-state predictors within the IP-Glasma+MUSIC+UrQMD framework. These predictors included the average initial entropy density
[s]
, the initial elliptic area
A=\pi r^{2}\sqrt{1-\varepsilon_{2}^{2}}=\pi\sqrt{4\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle y^{2}\rangle}
(where
\langle x^{2}\rangle,\langle y^{2}\rangle
are moments of the initial profile and
\varepsilon_{2}
is the eccentricity), and the initial squared radius
r^{2}
. The Pearson correlation coefficient used to quantify the correlation is defined as:

Q_{\xi}=\frac{\langle\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\,\delta\hat{\xi}\rangle}{\sqrt{\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\rangle\,\langle(\delta\hat{\xi})^{2}\rangle}}\,,

(5.1)

where
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]=[p_{\text{T}}]-\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
is the fluctuation of the event-wise average
p_{\text{T}}
around its ensemble mean
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, and
\delta\hat{\xi}
is the fluctuation of the considered initial-state predictor
\xi
.

Figure
5.4
shows that, in central collisions, all considered predictors correlate similarly with
[p_{\text{T}}]
. With decreasing multiplicity (more peripheral collisions), the overlap region becomes more elliptic; incorporating eccentricity into an entropy-per-area predictor dramatically improves its performance across centralities. In peripheral collisions where
\varepsilon_{2}\sim 1
and the region may fragment into multiple “hot spots”, the average initial entropy density
[s]
is found to be the most reliable predictor. The total initial energy at fixed multiplicity yields correlations comparable to
[s]
, except in the most peripheral events, and an energy-per-area predictor
E/A
performs marginally better than an entropy-per-area predictor
S/A
. Therefore, state-of-the-art model calculations point towards the origin of final-state
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations as arising from initial-state fluctuations in size or energy/entropy density.

Figure 5.4
:
Pearson correlation coefficients
Q_{\xi}
for various single-term initial-state predictors
\xi
of event-wise average transverse momentum
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5.02\ \mathrm{TeV}
, from Ref.
[
24
]
.

Thus, the measurement of fluctuations in
[p_{\text{T}}]
using final-state particles is expected to provide a means to experimentally: (1) Determine whether radial flow fluctuations arise as a collective response to initial-state fluctuations, and, (2) Constrain initial-state fluctuations in size and energy or entropy density.

Similar to anisotropic flow coefficients
v_{n}
, radial flow fluctuations can be characterized in two main ways. The first is a
p_{\text{T}}
-integrated measure, typically defined over a specific range of
p_{\text{T}}
:

v_{0}=\frac{\sqrt{\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\rangle}}{\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}\,,

(5.2)

which quantifies the relative standard deviation of
[p_{\text{T}}]

[
128
,
126
,
31
]
.

The second way is to measure radial flow fluctuations in a
p_{\text{T}}
-differential manner, analogous to
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
. To understand this approach, we first consider how variations in the event-wise
[p_{\text{T}}]
influence the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra. Assuming that an event’s
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum follows a simplified Boltzmann-like distribution
f(p_{\text{T}})\propto p_{T}e^{(-p_{T}/T_{\rm eff})}
, the mean
p_{\text{T}}
for that event is
[p_{\text{T}}]=2T_{\rm eff}
, where
T_{\rm eff}
is the effective temperature (inverse slope parameter of the spectra). Therefore, for events with the same multiplicity, an increase in
[p_{\text{T}}]
(i.e., a larger
T_{\rm eff}
) corresponds to a flatter
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum. This is schematically depicted in Figure
5.5
: the black solid line shows the ensemble-averaged spectrum, while the blue dashed line represents an event with a larger
[p_{\text{T}}]
(and thus larger
T_{\rm eff}
).

A key observation from Figure
5.5
is that, for events with a given multiplicity, the spectrum of an event with a higher
[p_{\text{T}}]
will cross the ensemble-averaged spectrum at
p_{\text{T}}=\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
of the full spectrum, denoted as
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{\text{cross}}
. Specifically, if
[p_{\text{T}}]
is higher than the ensemble average
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, the particle yield
n(p_{\text{T}})=\frac{dN(p_{\text{T}})/dp_{T}}{\int N(p_{\text{T}})}
will be suppressed at low
p_{\text{T}}
i.e.
p_{\text{T}}<\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{\text{cross}}
and enhanced at high
p_{\text{T}}
, i.e.
p_{\text{T}}>\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{\text{cross}}
relative to the average spectrum. Consequently, the covariance of the event-wise fluctuation in particle yield
\delta n(p_{\text{T}})=n(p_{\text{T}})-\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle
with the fluctuation in the event-wise mean
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
is expected to be negative for
p_{\text{T}}<\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{\text{cross}}
and positive for
p_{\text{T}}>\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{\text{cross}}
. This motivates the definition of the
p_{\text{T}}
-differential radial flow fluctuation observable,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})

[
24
,
25
]
.

Figure 5.5
:
Schematic illustration of radial flow fluctuations. The particle yield
n(p_{\text{T}})
for an event with large radial flow (larger
[p_{\text{T}}]
, blue dashed line) and small radial flow (smaller
[p_{\text{T}}]
, red dotted line) are shown relative to the ensemble-average spectrum (black solid line). Positive covariance
\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\,\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle>0
is expected at
p_{\text{T}}\gtrsim\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle_{\text{cross}}
, and negative covariance at
p_{\text{T}}\lesssim\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle_{\text{cross}}

[
25
]
.

Mathematically,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is defined via the relation:

\frac{\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\,\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}{\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\,\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}=v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})\,v_{0}\,.

(5.3)

This formulation factorizes the normalized covariance into the
p_{\text{T}}
-integrated fluctuation
v_{0}
and the
p_{\text{T}}
-dependent measure
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
.

To determine if
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is collective, we examine whether it exhibits characteristics similar to the anisotropic flow harmonics
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
, whose collective nature is well-established through various experimental signatures.

5.1.1.2
Hallmarks of Collectivity for Anisotropic Flow
v_{n}

To establish the collective origin of
v_{n}
, several experimental signatures are typically examined. These provide a template for assessing the collective nature of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
as well. Key signatures include:

1.

Factorization of
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
:

A crucial signature of collective behavior is the factorization, where the two-particle correlations can be approximated by the product of single-particle anisotropy coefficients. For instance, the two-particle correlation
V_{nn}(p_{Ta},p_{Tb})
for particles
a
and
b
with transverse momenta
p_{Ta}
and
p_{Tb}
can be approximately expressed as:

V_{nn}(p_{Ta},p_{Tb})\approx v_{n}(p_{Ta})v_{n}(p_{Tb})+\text{non-flow terms}\,,

(5.4)

where
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
is the
n^{\text{th}}
-order single-particle flow coefficients
[
129
,
26
]
. If the anisotropy arises from a common collective response to the event geometry,
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
measured this way should be consistent across different particle pairings and kinematic regions, once non-flow effects are accounted for.

Figure 5.6
:
Comparison of
v_{2}(p_{\text{T}})
as a function of
p_{\text{T}}
from mixed-
p_{\text{T}}
correlations using different analysis methods for the 20–30% centrality interval in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=2.76\ \mathrm{TeV}
. Error bars indicate statistical uncertainties only
[
26
]
.

Figure
5.6
illustrates this factorization for elliptic flow (
v_{2}
) in Pb+Pb collisions, where
v_{2}(p_{\text{T}})
extracted using different methods shows consistency, supporting a common collective origin.

2.

Long-range correlations in rapidity:

Collective flow is characterized by correlations that extend over a large range in pseudorapidity. This long-range nature distinguishes it from short-range correlations (e.g., from resonance decays or jet fragmentation). The initial anisotropy of the collision zone is translated into momentum anisotropies via the medium’s expansion, affecting particles across a broad rapidity span.

Figure
5.7
shows a two-particle azimuthal correlation function
C(\Delta\phi,\Delta\eta)
, where a ridge-like structure at
\Delta\phi\approx 0
(and
\pi
for
v_{2}
) extends over a large
\Delta\eta
range, providing evidence for a long-range collective phenomena.

Figure 5.7
:
Two-dimensional two-particle correlation function
C(\Delta\phi,\Delta\eta)
for Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=2.76\ \mathrm{TeV}
in the 0-5% centrality class, showing long-range ‘ridge’
[
26
]
.

3.

Centrality independence of hydrodynamic response
k_{n}
=
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{n}
:

In a hydrodynamically driven expansion, the anisotropic flow coefficients
v_{n}
are expected to be proportional to the initial spatial anisotropies
\varepsilon_{n}
, i.e.,
v_{n}=\kappa_{n}\varepsilon_{n}
. While
v_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
vary with collision centrality, their ratio
\kappa_{n}
(the hydrodynamic response) is less sensitive. Therefore, the shape of
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
, when scaled by the
p_{\text{T}}
-integrated
v_{n}
(i.e.,
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{n}
), is expected to show a weak dependence on centrality if the system’s response is universal. Figure
5.8
(Right) presents
v_{2}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{2}
versus a scaled
p_{\text{T}}
, showing that curves for different centralities approximately overlap, suggesting centrality independence of the hydrodynamic response coefficient.

Figure 5.8
:
(Left) The elliptic flow coefficient
v_{2}(p_{\text{T}})
and (Right) its normalized form
v_{2}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{2}
are shown as functions of the scaled transverse momentum,
\frac{p_{T}}{\overline{p}_{T}}\,,
where
\overline{p}_{T}
is the mean
p_{\text{T}}
of all particles in the analyzed
p_{\text{T}}
range. Error bars represent the combined statistical and systematic uncertainties
[
27
]
.

4.

Particle Identity (PID) dependence of
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
:

A well-established feature of anisotropic flow is the mass ordering of
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
: at low
p_{\text{T}}
,
v_{n}
is larger for lighter particles (e.g., pions) than for heavier ones (e.g., protons). This is attributed to the effect of a common radial flow velocity field boosting particles, leading to heavier particles gaining more momentum but exhibiting smaller
v_{n}
at a given low
p_{\text{T}}
. At higher
p_{\text{T}}
(typically
>2
GeV), a reverse ordering or grouping by particle type (meson/baryon) can occur. This PID dependence provides important insights into the collective expansion and hadronization dynamics.
Recent predictions for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
also suggest a distinct mass ordering
[
25
]
, where
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(or its magnitude) is expected to decrease with increasing particle mass at a given
p_{\text{T}}
, reflecting how particles of different masses are affected by fluctuations in the effective temperature or radial expansion.

To establish the collective nature of
p_{\text{T}}
-differential radial flow fluctuations, this work will present evidence for analogous signatures for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
. Specifically, we will investigate properties related to the first three hallmarks using data from the ATLAS experiment
[
130
]
. The ATLAS detector has limited particle identification capabilities for charged hadrons, so the fourth signature (PID dependence) cannot be verified in this analysis; however, a complementary study by the ALICE Collaboration has addressed this aspect
[
131
]
.

5.1.2
Previous Studies

Previous experimental studies on radial flow fluctuations have primarily focused on moments of the event-by-event mean transverse momentum distribution,
[p_{\text{T}}]
, such as the ensemble mean
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle

[
132
]
and the scaled variance
v_{0}=\sqrt{\left\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\right\rangle}/\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle

[
128
]
. Higher-order cumulants, such as the skewness and kurtosis of the
[p_{\text{T}}]
distribution, have also been investigated
[
133
,
134
]
. However, a dedicated observable for a differential study of radial flow fluctuations as a function of particle
p_{\text{T}}
, analogous to the
p_{\text{T}}
-differential anisotropic flow coefficients
v_{n}(p_{\text{T}})
, has been lacking.

To address this gap, the observable
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
was proposed and theoretically investigated
[
24
]
, with further developments and studies in subsequent works
[
31
,
25
]
. By correlating event-by-event fluctuations in the shape of the particle
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum with variations in the event-by-event mean transverse momentum,
[p_{\text{T}}]
, the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
observable enables a
p_{\text{T}}
-differential study of radial flow fluctuations.

This chapter presents the first measurement of the transverse-momentum dependence of radial flow fluctuations,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, over the range
0.5<p_{T}<10
GeV. The measurement utilizes a two-particle correlation method and is performed using Pb+Pb collision data at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=
5.02 TeV recorded by the ATLAS detector. The primary objectives of this study are to: (1) identify features in the measured
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
distribution indicative of collective expansion, and (2) compare the experimental results with predictions from state-of-the-art hydrodynamic models, to assess the sensitivity of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
to
\zeta/s
. Establishing these characteristics is important for validating the collective nature of radial flow and for leveraging its fluctuations to further constrain the transport properties of the QGP.

This chapter is organized as follows. Section
5.1.3
describes the analysis methodology. Section
5.1.4.5
presents cross-checks validating the measurement. Section
5.1.4
presents the main results, focusing on evidence for collectivity and comparisons with hydrodynamic calculations. Section
5.1.5
examines model studies of event classification effects. Finally, Section
5.1.6
summarizes the findings and Section
5.1.7
offers an outlook.

5.1.3
Methodology

We start with the measurement of cumulants of the event-wise mean transverse momentum,
[p_{\text{T}}]
, adapting techniques well-established in anisotropic flow analyses. This approach follows the standard multi-particle cumulant method, which typically involves the calculation of Q-vectors to quantify flow harmonics
[
100
,
135
,
136
]
. The underlying mathematical framework for calculating such cumulants is also well-established
[
101
,
102
]
. A key distinction for
[p_{\text{T}}]
cumulants is that the per-particle contribution to a Q-vector (e.g.,
e^{in\phi}
for anisotropic flow) is replaced by a scalar quantity representing the deviation of
[p_{\text{T}}]
in an event from the event ensemble average:
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
. Ensemble averages
\langle\dots\rangle
are calculated over events within narrow bins of event activity (such as
N_{\text{ch}}
or
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
). The results from these narrow bins are then combined to obtain averages for wider centrality ranges.

The
n
-particle
p_{\text{T}}
correlator, denoted
c_{n}
, is defined for a single event as:

\displaystyle c_{n}=\frac{\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i_{1}\neq...\neq i_{n}}w_{i_{1}}...w_{i_{n}}(p_{T,i_{1}}-\langle[p_{T}]\rangle)...(p_{T,i_{n}}-\langle[p_{T}]\rangle)}{\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i_{1}\neq...\neq i_{n}}w_{i_{1}}...w_{i_{n}}},

(5.5)

where
w_{i}
is the weight assigned to track
i
,
p_{T,i}
is its transverse momentum, and the sum is over all unique combinations of
n
distinct tracks within the event. Following the framework presented in Ref.
[
101
]
, these correlators can be efficiently computed using polynomial functions of weighted single-particle sums. Further details on the exact formulae used are provided in Appendix
6.7
.

Information about radial flow is encoded in the
p_{\text{T}}
-dependent yield in each event,
N(p_{\text{T}})
, which can be factorized into the product of the global multiplicity
N=\int N(p_{\text{T}})dp_{T}
and the normalized (fractional) spectrum,
n(p_{\text{T}})
, such that
N(p_{\text{T}})=Nn(p_{\text{T}})
. The event-by-event fluctuations in
N(p_{\text{T}})
can be decomposed into three terms
[
137
]
:

\delta N(p_{\text{T}})=\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\delta N+\langle N\rangle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})+\delta N\delta n(p_{\text{T}}).

(5.6)

In most cases, the higher-order term
\delta N\delta n(p_{\text{T}})
is negligible. Variations in global multiplicity
N
are related to centrality dependence, whereas, fluctuations in
n(p_{\text{T}})
capture genuine radial flow fluctuations related to the shape of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum. Using the normalized yield
n(p_{\text{T}})
emphasizes this shape, a key characteristic of radial flow as discussed in Section
5.1.1
, and suppresses trivial fluctuations related to variations in the overall event multiplicity.

To mitigate auto-correlations and non-flow, the two-subevent method is employed. Subevent ‘A’ covers
\eta\in(-2.5,-\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}/2)
and subevent ‘B’ covers
\eta\in(\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}/2,2.5)
. The analysis explores four different pseudorapidity gap sizes:
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=0,1,2,
and
3
. The
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
(and thus
v_{0}
) is calculated from subevent A, whereas the yield fluctuations
\delta n(p_{\text{T}})
are calculated from subevent B. The final covariance is obtained as a weighted average of the correlations from the two possible subevent pairings:
\langle\delta n_{\text{A}}(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{\text{B}}\rangle
and
\langle\delta n_{\text{B}}(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{\text{A}}\rangle
. The final
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measurement is then measured using:

\frac{\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\,\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}{\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\,\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}=v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})\,v_{0}\,.

(5.7)

The analysis is performed on data for Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=5.02
TeV, described in detail in Section
4.1
. Track reconstruction efficiency,
\epsilon(p_{T},\eta,N_{\text{ch}})
, and the rate of falsely reconstructed (“fake”) tracks,
f(p_{T},\eta,N_{\text{ch}})
, are determined using Monte Carlo (MC) simulations as detailed in Chapter
4
. The corrected charged particle multiplicity,
N_{\text{ch}}
, used for event classification, is calculated as the sum of the weights used for corrections,
\sum_{i}w_{i}=\sum_{i}\frac{1-f_{i}}{\epsilon_{i}}
, for particles within
0.5<p_{T}<5
GeV and
|\eta|<2.5
. Statistical uncertainties for all measured observables are estimated using a Poisson bootstrap method
[
138
,
139
]
. A further description of the Bootstrap method used can be found in Appendix
6.11
.

Systematic uncertainties in the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measurement arise from various detector effects and analysis procedures. Key contributions include those from track selection criteria, resulting in deviations of 0.5–2.5% for
v_{0}
and, for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, 2–7% at low
p_{\text{T}}
versus 0–1% at high
p_{\text{T}}
. Tracking efficiency and fake rate corrections, linked to detector material modeling in GEANT simulations, contribute 0–1% to
v_{0}
; for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, impacts are 0.5–1% at high
p_{\text{T}}
and 2–4% at low
p_{\text{T}}
, with negligible effect from azimuthal efficiency variations. Residual pileup effects lead to uncertainties below 0.5% for all measured observables. Centrality definition uncertainties, stemming from trigger inefficiencies in peripheral event sampling, are less than 1.5% and primarily affect centrality-binned results. An MC consistency check adds approximately 1% uncertainty to
v_{0}
and about 2% to
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
. Appendix
6.12
provides further details on the contributions from different systematic sources.

5.1.4
Results

To establish the collective nature of the observed
p_{T}
-differential radial flow fluctuations, this section presents experimental evidence for three key hallmarks analogous to those observed for
v_{n}
: (i) the persistence of
v_{0}(p_{T})
correlations over large pseudorapidity gaps, indicative of a long-range phenomenon; (ii) the factorization of
v_{0}(p_{T})
, implying its independence from the specific choice of reference transverse momentum (
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
) ranges; and (iii) a universal, centrality-independent shape of the normalized response,
v_{0}(p_{T})/v_{0}
. These features, if confirmed, would strongly support an early-time, bulk origin for these fluctuations, distinct from localized, non-collective effects.

5.1.4.1
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
Factorization

Figure
5.9
a illustrates the centrality dependence of
v_{0}
as computed in three distinct
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
ranges. While the absolute magnitude of
v_{0}
varies with the chosen
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
interval, its centrality trend exhibits remarkable consistency across all selections, as depicted in Figure
5.9
b using the ratio
v_{0}/v_{0}^{0-5\%}
(normalized to its value in the 0–5% centrality range). This observation suggests that the centrality-dependent shape of
v_{0}
captures the global, collective nature of radial flow fluctuations.

Figure 5.9
:
Centrality dependence of
v_{0}
(
a
) and
v_{0}/v_{0}^{0-5\%}
(
b
) for three
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
ranges. Panels (
c
) and (
d
) show, respectively, the normalized covariance in Eq.
5.7
, and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
in 0–5% centrality (top) and 50–60% centrality (bottom), each plotted for three
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
ranges. The lines indicate the corresponding predictions from the HIJING model containing only non-flow correlations. Bars and shaded areas indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.

Next, the factorization of the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is further verified in Figures
5.9
c and
5.9
d for two representative centrality ranges (0–5% and 50–60%). A key finding is that while the normalized covariance exhibits substantial variation with the chosen
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
interval, the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
remains remarkably independent of
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
up to
p_{\text{T}}\approx 3
GeV. Therefore,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is independent of the
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
range used for its measurement. This robust factorization behavior, analogous to that observed for anisotropic flow, strongly supports the collective origin of radial flow fluctuations.

In central collisions,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
exhibits a decrease above
p_{\text{T}}\approx 4
GeV, mirroring similar observations for anisotropic flow, which may indicate a transition from a flow-dominated to a jet-quenching dominated regime. This decrease is not observed in relatively peripheral collisions. Figures
5.9
c also present a comparison of the experimental data with the non-flow expectation from the HIJING model. The HIJING model calculations generally underpredict the covariance for
p_{\text{T}}<8
GeV, implying presence of genuine correlations in data, beyond those expected from a simple superposition scenario of independent nucleon-nucleon interactions.

However, it is important to note that when these normalized covariances are converted into
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
using Eq.
5.7
, HIJING can artificially inflate the apparent non-flow contribution
[
137
,
131
]
. A more reliable assessment of non-flow effects from models like HIJING is reliable only at the level of 2-particle covariance.

5.1.4.2
Long-Range Behavior

Another test for the collective origin of
v_{0}(p_{T})
is its persistence across large pseudorapidity separations,
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
, which suppresses contributions from short-range non-flow correlations such as jet fragments or resonance decays.

Variations of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
with
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
are shown in Figure
5.10
for 0–5% and 60–70% centralities. In central collisions, only minor changes are observed in
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
as
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
is increased from 0 to 3. In contrast, peripheral collisions (60–70%) show a slight reduction in
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
at
p_{\text{T}}>3
GeV with increasing
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
, consistent with the suppression of residual non-flow contributions, potentially from away-side jet fragmentation.

This weak dependence on
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
provides strong support for the interpretation that radial flow is characterized by long-range, global correlations, akin to anisotropic flow. Consequently, an
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=1
is adopted as the default configuration for the subsequent results.

Figure 5.10
:
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
for
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
=0, 1, 2, and 3 in 0–5% (left) and 60–70% (right) centrality. Bars and shaded areas indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.

The study also investigated the impact of the
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
on the separation between
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measured with varying
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
, as illustrated in Figure
5.11
. When varying the upper or lower limits of the
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
range, the differences between results obtained with different
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
values become more pronounced. This observation suggests that the choice of
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
range can influence the level of residual non-flow contributions captured by the measurement.

Figure 5.11
:
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
for
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
=0, 1, 2, and 3 in 0–5% (left) and 60–70% (right) centrality and for two
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
ranges, 0.5–5 GeV (top) and 1–5 GeV (bottom). Bars and shaded areas indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.

5.1.4.3
Centrality Dependence of Hydrodynamic Response

The centrality dependence of the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
shape is investigated next using the normalized quantity
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
. Figure
5.12
presents a comparison of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
across various centrality ranges. While the absolute magnitude of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
displays a pronounced centrality dependence, the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
exhibits a remarkable overlap for
p_{\text{T}}\lesssim 2.5
GeV. This observation indicates that the radial flow fluctuations are primarily driven by event-by-event fluctuations in the initial state. This observation also implies that the shape of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is governed by the hydrodynamic response,
k_{0}(p_{\text{T}})\approx v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}

[
25
]
, which appears to be largely independent of centrality. This scaling behavior is analogous to that observed for anisotropic flow.

Figure 5.12
:
The
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(left) and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
(right) for different centrality ranges. Bars and shaded areas represent statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.

Above
p_{\text{T}}\approx 3
GeV, where non-flow contamination becomes large, this shape scaling is not perfectly maintained. The characteristic rise and fall behavior of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
is observed up to 40–50% centrality, which could be attributed to the onset of jet quenching processes that reduce momentum fluctuations. For more peripheral collisions, however, the data show either a flatter behavior or an increasing trend with
p_{\text{T}}
, consistent with a larger non-flow contribution at high
p_{\text{T}}
and in these peripheral events.

Figure 5.13
:
The
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
(left) and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}}/\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}})/v_{0}
(right) for
0.5<p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}<2
GeV in 0–5% centrality range, where
\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}}=0.73
GeV is the mean
p_{\text{T}}
of charged particle spectrum measured by the ALICE Collaboration
[
28
]
. They are compared with hydrodynamic model predictions without viscosity (red), with constant shear viscosity (in terms of shear viscosity to entropy density ratio of
\eta/s=\frac{1}{4\pi}=0.08
, magenta), and with temperature dependent bulk viscosity (blue).

5.1.4.4
Sensitivity to
\zeta/s

An advantage of
v_{0}(p_{T})
as a probe of QGP transport properties lies in its pronounced sensitivity to the bulk viscosity to entropy density ratio,
\zeta/s
, while exhibiting comparatively weaker dependence on shear viscosity,
\eta/s

[
25
]
.

Figure
5.13
presents a comparison of the experimental data in the 0–5% centrality range with predictions from a hydrodynamic model described in Ref.
[
25
]
. These predictions are based on the IP-Glasma+MUSIC framework
[
40
]
and incorporate either a constant shear viscosity or a temperature-dependent bulk viscosity.

As these calculations are not fully tuned to precisely match the overall
p_{\text{T}}
spectra or event-ensemble
p_{\text{T}}
fluctuations, Refs.
[
25
,
28
]
recommend utilizing dimensionless ratios such as
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}}/\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}})/v_{0}
. Here,
\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}}
represents the average transverse momentum of the inclusive charged-particle spectrum. Using these ratios helps to reduce dependencies on initial conditions and to further mitigate model dependencies on the overall spectral shape.

Calculations performed with and without shear viscosity yield similar predictions for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
, as shown in Figure
5.13
. In contrast, the inclusion of a temperature-dependent bulk viscosity alters both the zero-crossing point in
p_{\text{T}}
and the high-
p_{\text{T}}
behavior. Without bulk viscosity, the model systematically underestimates the data at high
p_{\text{T}}
. However, with the addition of bulk viscosity, the predictions move closer to the experimental data, although they remain slightly above it. Rescaling the horizontal axis by
\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}}
further enhances the agreement in the region
p_{\text{T}}/\bar{p}_{\mathrm{T}}<3
, highlighting the pronounced impact of bulk viscosity on the
p_{\text{T}}
-differential radial flow. While the current calculations still require further tuning, this comparison clearly demonstrates the potential of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
data to provide valuable constraints on bulk viscosity of QGP.

5.1.4.5
Cross-Checks

To ensure the robustness and validity of the measured
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and the interpretation of its collective nature, a series of detailed cross-checks were performed. These include the verification of sum rules for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and the investigation of the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
crossing point.

5.1.4.5.1  Verification of Sum-Rules for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})

There are two mathematical sum rules that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is expected to follow:

\displaystyle\int v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})N_{0}(p_{\text{T}})dp_{T}=0

(5.8)

\displaystyle\int(p_{T}-\langle p_{T}\rangle)v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})N_{0}(p_{\text{T}})dp_{T}=\sigma_{p_{T}}N_{0}

(5.9)

where
N_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is the inclusive
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum,
\langle p_{T}\rangle
is the mean of the inclusive spectrum, and
\sigma_{p_{T}}
is its standard deviation. Further discussions on these formulae can be found in Appendix
6.7
.

The first sum rule in Eq.
5.8
implies that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
must change sign as a function of
p_{\text{T}}
. The measured
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(e.g., in Figure
5.12
) indeed exhibits a characteristic sign change, typically crossing zero near the average
p_{\text{T}}
of the inclusive spectrum. The second sum rule in Eq.
5.9
provides a quantitative relation between the
p_{\text{T}}
-differential
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and the
p_{\text{T}}
-integrated fluctuation magnitude
v_{0}
, which is expected to hold if the radial flow fluctuations are collective
[
24
,
25
]
.

Figure
5.14
presents the closure for Sum Rules 1 (Eq.
5.8
) and 2 (Eq.
5.9
). Sum Rule 1 exhibits excellent closure, with deviations within
0.5\%
. In contrast, Sum Rule 2, shows a small non-closure of approximately 5%. This level of agreement is within the systematic uncertainty on the direct measurement of
v_{0}
, indicating reasonable consistency.

Minor deviations are observed in peripheral centralities, potentially due to non-flow effects. The relatively larger non-closure in Sum Rule 2 likely stems from the fact that this sum rule is approximate; it holds exactly only in the absence of non-flow effects and under the assumption of perfect factorization of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
. Given that these ideal conditions are not fully met across the entire kinematic range of
0.5<p_{\text{T}}<10
GeV, the observed deviations are expected.

Figure 5.14
:
Fractional closure for Sum Rules 1 (Eq
5.8
) and 2 (Eq
5.9
) is shown across different reference
p_{\text{T}}
ranges in the left and middle columns, respectively. The right column compares the
v_{0}
values obtained from Sum Rule 2 with those from the two-subevent method. Each row corresponds to a different
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
value:
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=0
(top) and
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=1
(bottom). Error bars represent statistical uncertainties, and the shaded bands indicate systematic uncertainties.

5.1.4.5.2  Crossing-Point of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})

As discussed earlier,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
crosses zero at
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra
[
24
]
. In this analysis, the
p_{\text{T}}
spectrum is measured within the range
0.5<p_{\text{T}}<10
GeV. If the origin of radial flow is collective, the crossing points of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
for different
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
should be consistent.

Figure
5.15
examines this consistency for
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=1
. For comparison, the mean transverse momentum
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
for particles within
0.5<p_{\text{T}}<10
GeV is also shown. The results demonstrate that, within systematic uncertainties, the crossing points of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
remain consistent across different
p^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathrm{T}}
and agree with the
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
across all centralities. Minor deviations observed in peripheral centralities may be attributed to non-flow effects.

Figure 5.15
:
The crossing point of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is plotted as a function of centrality for different reference
p_{\text{T}}
ranges, with results shown for
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=1
. The solid line represents
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
for tracks within
0.5<p_{\text{T}}<10
GeV. Error bars indicate statistical uncertainties, while shaded bands represent systematic uncertainties.

5.1.5
HIJING Study on Effects of Multiplicity Fluctuations and Acceptance

In the data analysis presented in previous sections, events were categorized based on different measures of event activity, such as charged particle multiplicity (
N_{\text{ch}}
) or forward transverse energy (
\sum E_{T}
). The choice of this event classifier, along with specific kinematic selections, are observed to influence the measured
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
. Consequently, a precise understanding of these effects is vital for an accurate interpretation of the experimental findings presented earlier.

5.1.5.1
Motivation

Certain features observed for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
presented in Section
5.1.4
warrant a dedicated model study to unravel the underlying factors. Specifically, these are:

Figure 5.16
:
Comparison of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measured using un-normalized event-by-event spectra
N(p_{\text{T}})
(open markers) and normalized spectra
n(p_{\text{T}})
(filled markers) for several centrality selections. Event averages are obtained by combining observables calculated in narrow slices of
N_{\text{ch}}
(circles) or
\sum E_{T}
(squares). They are obtained for
p_{T,\text{ref}}
in
0.5
–
2\text{~GeV}
and
\eta_{\text{gap}}=1
. Bars and shaded areas indicate statistical and total uncertainties, respectively.

1.

Dependence on Event Classifier and Spectra Normalization:

Experimental measurements of
v_{0}(p_{T})
revealed differing results depending on whether centrality was defined using
\sum E_{T}
or
N_{\text{ch}}
. These differences were pronounced when
v_{0}(p_{T})
was calculated using un-normalized particle spectra,
N(p_{T})
. As illustrated in Figure
5.16
, calculations employing normalized spectra,
n(p_{T})=N(p_{T})/N
(where
N
is the total multiplicity within the considered acceptance), yield consistent
v_{0}(p_{T})
values for both
\sum E_{T}
and
N_{\text{ch}}
-based centrality definitions. In contrast, when using un-normalized spectra
N(p_{T})
:

-

v_{0}(p_{T})
derived from an
N_{\text{ch}}
-based centrality definition is smaller than the
v_{0}(p_{T})
obtained using
n(p_{T})
with the same centrality definition.

-

Furthermore,
v_{0}(p_{T})
derived from a FCal
\sum E_{T}
-based centrality definition and
N(p_{T})
is larger than results obtained with
N_{\text{ch}}
-based centrality (for both
N(p_{T})
and
n(p_{T})
calculations).

These choices also led to variations in the zero-crossing point of
v_{0}(p_{T})
.

2.

Influence of Kinematic
p_{T}
Range for Spectral Definition:

The specific transverse momentum (
p_{T}
) range selected to define the spectra (
N(p_{T})
or
n(p_{T})
) was observed to influence the resulting
v_{0}(p_{T})
. This is partly attributed to the relationship between the zero-crossing point of
v_{0}(p_{T})
and the average
p_{T}
of the spectrum under study; modifying the
p_{T}
range alters this average
p_{T}
and consequently the behavior of
v_{0}(p_{T})
.

3.

Ambiguity in Existing Model Interpretations:

A key theoretical study discussing
v_{0}(p_{T})

[
25
]
did not explicitly detail the comparative effects of using un-normalized spectra
N(p_{T})
versus normalized spectra
n(p_{T})
in the calculation of
v_{0}(p_{T})
, leaving an interpretational gap regarding experimental data.

To investigate these observations and clarify the role of non-flow effects, multiplicity fluctuations, and analysis choices, we employ the HIJING event generator
[
103
,
104
]
. HIJING lacks genuine collective radial flow, making it suitable for studying non-collective baseline effects.

5.1.5.2
Methodology

The
p_{T}
-differential radial flow
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is fundamentally defined via the covariance of spectral fluctuations with fluctuations in the event-wise mean transverse momentum
[p_{\text{T}}]
:

\frac{\langle\delta X(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}{\langle X(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle_{A}}=v_{0}^{(X)}(p_{\text{T}})v_{0,[p_{\text{T}}]},

(5.10)

where
X(p_{\text{T}})
can be either the normalized spectrum
n(p_{\text{T}})
or the un-normalized spectrum
N(p_{\text{T}})
, leading to
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
or
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
respectively. The term
v_{0,[p_{\text{T}}]}=\sqrt{\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\rangle_{A}}/\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle_{A}
quantifies the relative fluctuation of
[p_{\text{T}}]
calculated within a specific reference
p_{T}
range
A
(denoted
p_{T,\text{ref}}
).

The
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
can also be expressed using the Pearson correlation coefficient
\rho
:

v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})=\rho(n(p_{\text{T}}),[p_{\text{T}}])\frac{\sqrt{\langle(\delta n(p_{\text{T}}))^{2}\rangle}}{\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle}.

(5.11)

This highlights that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
isolates the part of the spectral shape fluctuation in
\delta n(p_{\text{T}})
that is linearly correlated with
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
. The magnitude
|v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})|\leq\sqrt{\langle(\delta n(p_{\text{T}}))^{2}\rangle}/\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle
. This correlation can be reduced by decorrelation effects, e.g., if
[p_{\text{T}}]
is measured at low
p_{T}
and
n(p_{\text{T}})
fluctuations are probed at much higher
p_{T}
.

5.1.5.2.1  Effect of Using un-normalized Spectra
N(p_{\text{T}})

If
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
is defined using the un-normalized spectrum
N(p_{\text{T}})
as per Eq.
5.10
, its difference from
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(defined with
n(p_{\text{T}})
) can be approximated as follows. Assuming
N(p_{\text{T}})=N\cdot n(p_{\text{T}})
and that fluctuations
\delta N
and
\delta n(p_{\text{T}})
are not strongly correlated (i.e.,
\langle\delta N\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\approx 0
, making
\langle N(p_{\text{T}})\rangle\approx\langle N\rangle\langle n(p_{\text{T}})\rangle
), the difference
\Delta v_{0}\equiv v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})-v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
is given by:

\Delta v_{0}\approx\frac{\langle\delta N\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle}{\langle N\rangle\sqrt{\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\rangle}}=\rho(N,[p_{\text{T}}])\frac{\sqrt{\langle(\delta N)^{2}\rangle}}{\langle N\rangle}.

(5.12)

This offset
\Delta v_{0}
depends on the relative fluctuation of the total multiplicity
N
within the considered acceptance and its correlation
\rho(N,[p_{\text{T}}])
with
[p_{\text{T}}]
. The sign of
\Delta v_{0}
is determined by
\rho(N,[p_{\text{T}}])
, which is sensitive to the event activity classifier used.

5.1.5.2.2  Effect of Restricted
p_{T}
Acceptance for Normalization

Experiments typically measure particles within a restricted
p_{T}
range, say
R
. If the fractional spectrum is defined only within this range,
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})=N(p_{\text{T}})/N_{R}
(where
N_{R}=\int_{R}N(p_{\text{T}})dp_{T}
), this introduces further effects. Even if the multiplicity
N_{\text{full}}
over a very wide range is fixed,
N_{R}
fluctuates due to the stochastic nature of particle population in the subrange
R
. For fixed
N_{\text{full}}
,
N_{R}
follows a binomial distribution with sampling probability
\epsilon=\langle N_{R}\rangle/\langle N_{\text{full}}\rangle
.

Let
v_{0,\text{full}}(p_{\text{T}})
be the observable defined using
n_{\text{full}}(p_{\text{T}})
(normalized by
N_{\text{full}}
) and
v_{0,R}(p_{\text{T}})
be defined using
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})
(normalized by
N_{R}
). The difference
\Delta v_{0,R}=v_{0,R}(p_{\text{T}})-v_{0,\text{full}}(p_{\text{T}})
arises. If
N_{\text{full}}
is fixed, this offset is primarily due to
N_{R}
fluctuations:

\Delta v_{0,R}\approx-\left(\rho(N_{R},[p_{\text{T}}])\sqrt{\frac{\langle(\delta N_{R})^{2}\rangle_{N}}{\langle N_{R}\rangle^{2}}}\right)_{N_{\text{full}}},

(5.13)

where the subscript
N_{\text{full}}
indicates evaluation at fixed total multiplicity. Using the binomial property
\langle(\delta N_{R})^{2}\rangle_{N_{\text{full}}}=\langle N_{R}\rangle(1-\epsilon)
:

\Delta v_{0,R}\approx-\left(\rho(N_{R},[p_{\text{T}}])\sqrt{\frac{1-\epsilon}{\langle N_{R}\rangle}}\right)_{N_{\text{full}}}.

(5.14)

This shift can alter the zero-crossing point of
v_{0,R}(p_{\text{T}})
, typically moving it towards
\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle_{R}
, the average
p_{T}
within the range
R
. This is illustrated schematically in Figure
5.17
(bottom panel). It is important that this renormalization primarily affects the crossing point, while the fundamental factorization property of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(its independence from
p_{T,\text{ref}}
for
v_{0,[p_{\text{T}}]}
) is expected to hold.

Figure 5.17
:
Top: Schematic illustration of how event-by-event (
EbE
) radial flow fluctuations can create correlations between the
p_{T}
-differential yield
n(p_{\text{T}})
and the
EbE
average transverse momentum
[p_{\text{T}}]
. The blue (red) curve represents an event with larger (smaller) than average radial flow, and the black curve is the ensemble-averaged spectrum. Bottom: Fractional spectra defined over a wide range
F
(e.g.,
n_{F}(p_{\text{T}})=N(p_{\text{T}})/N_{F}
) versus a subrange
R
(e.g.,
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})=N(p_{\text{T}})/N_{R}
) are normalized differently, affecting
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
calculations.

To study these effects, Pb+Pb collisions were generated using HIJING (version with quenching, HIPR1(10)=2 GeV for the hard scale cutoff) at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5.02
TeV. Charged particles with
|\eta|<2.5
and
p_{T}<10
GeV were used. For two-subevent calculations, particles were divided into subevent A (
-2.5<\eta_{a}<-\eta_{\text{gap}}/2
) and subevent B (
\eta_{\text{gap}}/2<\eta_{b}<2.5
), with
\eta_{\text{gap}}=0
and
3
explored.
The event-wise
[p_{\text{T}}]
was calculated using reference particles in three
p_{T,\text{ref}}
ranges:
0.5
–
2
GeV,
0.5
–
5
GeV, and
1
–
5
GeV. The spectra
N(p_{\text{T}})
or
n(p_{\text{T}})
, and subsequently
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
or
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
, were determined in three overall
p_{T}
analysis ranges:
0
–
10
GeV (full range),
0.5
–
10
GeV (subrange
R_{1}
), and
1
–
10
GeV (subrange
R_{2}
).
Events were classified based on various definitions of charged particle multiplicity (
N_{\text{ch}}
) in different
\eta
windows, and by the number of participant nucleons (
N_{\text{part}}
), as detailed in Table
5.1
.

Table 5.1
:
Kinematic selections for particles used in defining
n(p_{\text{T}})
or
N(p_{\text{T}})
, for calculating
[p_{\text{T}}]
(via
p_{T,\text{ref}}
), and for event activity classification in the HIJING study.

p_{T}
range for
N(p_{\text{T}})
,
n(p_{\text{T}})

p_{T,\text{ref}}
for
[p_{\text{T}}]
(GeV)

Event Activity Classifier
