–
10
GeV,
|\eta|<2.5

0.5
–
2

N_{\text{ch}}
in
|\eta|<2.5

0.5
–
10
GeV,
|\eta|<2.5

0.5
–
5

N_{\text{ch}}
in
2.5<|\eta|<3.2

1
–
10
GeV,
|\eta|<2.5

1
–
5

N_{\text{ch}}
in
3.2<|\eta|<4

N_{\text{ch}}
in
4<|\eta|<5

N_{\text{part}}

For each event,
[p_{\text{T}}]
,
N(p_{\text{T}})
, and
n(p_{\text{T}})
were calculated for each subevent. Two-particle cumulants were used for ensemble averages:
\langle(\delta[p_{\text{T}}])^{2}\rangle=\langle\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle
, and
\langle\delta X(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\delta X_{a}(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}+\delta X_{b}(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{a}\rangle
.
The offset term
\Delta v_{0}
(Eq.
5.12
) was estimated using the two-subevent method as:

\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}=\frac{\langle\delta N_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle}{\langle N_{a}\rangle\sqrt{\langle\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle}}.

(5.15)

This analysis procedure mirrors that used in the ATLAS measurements
[
130
]
.

Since HIJING is based on an independent particle production source model, various quantities are expected to follow simple scaling laws with the number of sources
N_{s}
. For instance, if particles for event activity selection are independent of those in the analysis:

\displaystyle\langle\delta N_{a}\delta N_{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\propto N_{s}

\displaystyle\langle\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\propto 1/N_{s}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\langle(\delta N_{a}\delta N_{b}\rangle}/\langle N_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle\propto 1/\sqrt{N_{s}}

\displaystyle\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}

\displaystyle\propto 1/\sqrt{N_{s}}

\displaystyle\langle\delta N_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim\text{const}

\displaystyle\rho(N_{a},[p_{\text{T}}]_{b})

\displaystyle\sim\text{const}

\displaystyle\sqrt{N_{s}}\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}

\displaystyle\sim\text{const}

(5.16)

where “const” implies independence from
N_{s}
.

5.1.5.3
Results

Figure
5.18
shows
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
from HIJING using
n(p_{\text{T}})
for three
p_{T,\text{ref}}
selections in central collisions. Non-flow correlations aare oberved to extend to low
p_{T}
and also cause variations in the zero-crossing point (see inset). The overall magnitude of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
in HIJING is much smaller than in experimental data, supporting the interpretation that collective radial flow, absent in HIJING, dominates the experimental measurements.

Figure 5.18
:
The
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
calculated from HIJING using fractional spectra
n(p_{\text{T}})
(normalized in
0
–
10
GeV range) for three
p_{T,\text{ref}}
selections, in
0
–
5\%
most central Pb+Pb collisions. The inset provides a zoomed-in view of the low
p_{T}
region.

5.1.5.3.1  Influence of Event Classification and Spectral Normalization

Figure
5.19
(a) presents
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
, calculated using un-normalized spectra
N(p_{\text{T}})
, for various event activity selections. The results exhibit similar increasing trends with
p_{T}
but show substantial vertical offsets relative to each other. These offsets, dependent on the event classifier, drastically alter the zero-crossing point. For event classes based on
N_{\text{part}}
or forward
N_{\text{ch}}
,
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
may not cross zero at all, indicating a strong influence of local spectral fluctuations with global multiplicity fluctuations.

In contrast, Figure
5.19
(b) shows
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(from normalized spectra
n(p_{\text{T}})
). These results cluster closely together for all event classifiers, sharing a common zero-crossing point near the average
p_{T}
of the inclusive spectrum. This demonstrates that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
from
n(p_{\text{T}})
is less sensitive to the choice of event classifier.
Figure
5.19
(c) shows that the differences between
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
in panel (a) and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
in panel (b) are largely accounted for by the calculated offset
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
from Eq.
5.15
, effectively reconciling the two definitions.

Figure 5.19
:
HIJING results for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
in
0
–
5\%
most central Pb+Pb collisions: (a)
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
obtained using
N(p_{\text{T}})
; (b)
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
obtained using
n(p_{\text{T}})
; (c)
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})
after applying the correction
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
from Eq.
5.15
. Different markers correspond to different event activity selections.

The validity of the
\Delta v_{0}
correction is further tested in Figure
5.20
. Panel (a) compares the measured difference
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})-v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(points) with the predicted
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
(dotted lines) versus
p_{T}
. The difference is nearly
p_{T}
-independent and well described by
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
. Panel (b) shows the centrality dependence of this difference at
p_{T}=1
GeV, scaled by
\sqrt{N_{\text{part}}}
. Again, remarkable agreement is observed.

Figure 5.20
:
Top (a): Comparison of
v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})-v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(points) with the estimated
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
(dotted lines) in
0
–
5\%
central HIJING Pb+Pb collisions. Bottom (b): Centrality dependence of
\sqrt{N_{\text{part}}}(v_{0}^{\prime}(p_{\text{T}})-v_{0}(p_{\text{T}}))
at
p_{T}=1
GeV (points) compared with
\sqrt{N_{\text{part}}}\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
(dotted lines).

These findings confirm that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
calculated with
n(p_{\text{T}})
effectively separates global multiplicity fluctuations from
p_{T}
-dependent spectral shape fluctuations. The correction term
\Delta v_{0}
increases with the
\eta
gap between analyzed particles and those used for event classification, consistent with known multiplicity decorrelation effects
[
140
,
141
,
142
]
, which can be linked to initial-state longitudinal fluctuations
[
143
]
.

The expected scaling laws in Eq.
5.16
from the independent source picture in HIJING were also tested. Figure
5.21
shows that quantities like
\langle\delta N_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle
and
\sqrt{N_{\text{part}}}\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
are approximately constant or vary slowly with centrality, supporting the theoretical expectations when event selection and analysis regions are sufficiently separated.

However, when the particles for event activity definition strongly overlap with those for analysis (e.g.,
N_{\text{ch}}
in
|\eta|<2.5
, blue symbols in Fig.
5.21
), observables like
\langle\delta N_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle
become small. This is due to kinematic constraints: fixing total
N_{\text{ch}}=N_{a}+N_{b}+N_{c}
(where
N_{c}
is in the gap) induces anticorrelations between
N_{a},N_{b},N_{c}
. This anticorrelation is strongest for
\eta_{\text{gap}}=0
(
N_{c}=0
) and weakens as
\eta_{\text{gap}}
increases. For well-separated regions or
N_{\text{part}}
-based selection, correlations are small or slightly positive, with magnitudes depending on the
\eta
range and increasing towards peripheral collisions for
N_{\text{part}}
selection. This behavior helps explain the ordering of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
observed experimentally for different centrality definitions as observed in ATLAS measurements and shown in Figure
5.16
.

Figure 5.21
:
Components of
\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
from HIJING:
\langle\delta N_{a}\delta[p_{\text{T}}]_{b}\rangle
(top),
\rho(N_{a},[p_{\text{T}}]_{b})
(middle), and
\sqrt{N_{s}}\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
(here shown as
\sqrt{N_{\text{part}}}\Delta v_{0}^{\text{calc}}
, bottom) vs. centrality for
\eta_{\text{gap}}=0
(left) and
\eta_{\text{gap}}=3
(right), for various event activity classifiers.

5.1.5.3.2  Consequence of Varying
p_{T}
Acceptance for Normalization

As discussed in Section
5.1.5.2.2
, normalizing spectra within a restricted
p_{T}
acceptance
R
(i.e., using
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})=N(p_{\text{T}})/N_{R}
) can lead to an offset
\Delta v_{0,R}
(Eqs.
5.13
-(
5.14
)) compared to using a wider normalization range. This primarily affects the zero-crossing point of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
.

Figure
5.22
(a) shows
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
from HIJING where
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})
was calculated using three different
p_{T}
ranges
R
for
N_{R}
. The results clearly depend on the chosen range
R
. Figure
5.22
(b) shows that these differences are largely reconciled after applying a correction for this acceptance-induced offset shown in Eq.
5.13
. This confirms that the precise zero-crossing location of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
in such cases is strongly influenced by the kinematic properties of the spectrum within the chosen acceptance
R
, rather than being a pure indicator of dynamical feature across the full spectrum.

Figure 5.22
:
(Left) HIJING
v_{0}(p_{T})
calculated using spectra
n_{R}(p_{\text{T}})
normalized by
N_{R}=\int_{A}^{B}N(p_{T})\,dp_{T}
where
R
corresponds to three different
p_{T}
ranges (
0
–
10
,
0.5
–
10
, and
1
–
10
GeV) in
0
–
5\%
most central collisions. (Right) The results after correcting for the acceptance-induced offset, showing improved consistency.

5.1.6
Conclusion

This chapter presents the first measurement of the transverse momentum dependence of radial flow fluctuations,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=5.02
TeV, performed by the ATLAS Collaboration
[
137
]
. The experimental data reveal strong evidence for the collective nature of radial flow, highlighted by three key features.
Firstly,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
exhibits minimal dependence on the pseudorapidity gap (
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
) indicating that radial flow is a global phenomenon extending over large longitudinal distances. Secondly, for
p_{\text{T}}\lesssim 3
GeV, the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
factrorizes, i.e it is independent of the specific
p^{ref}_{\mathrm{T}}
range used to define
v_{0}
, consistent with the factorization behavior observed for
v_{n}
with
n\geq 2
. Thirdly, when normalized by the integrated fluctuation
v_{0}
, the shape of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
(i.e.,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
) is almost independent of collision centrality for
p_{\text{T}}\lesssim 2.5
GeV. This striking feature suggests a universal hydrodynamic response to radial expansion, with the magnitude of fluctuations primarily driven by initial-state geometry.

Comparisons with hydrodynamic model calculations show that the inclusion of a temperature-dependent bulk viscosity, parameterized according to lattice QCD expectations, significantly improves the description of the
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
data, especially concerning its zero-crossing point and high-
p_{\text{T}}
behavior. This provides a novel experimental avenue to quantitatively constrain
\zeta/s
, a transport coefficient that has proven more challenging to access than shear viscosity but is important for a complete understanding of QGP properties.

A detailed HIJING model study
[
137
]
demonstrated that
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measured using fractional spectra agree better between different centrality definitions, unlike
N(p_{\text{T}})
which exhibits shifts dependent on centrality definition. This difference is attributed to the trivial correlation between total multiplicity and average transverse momentum.
Furthermore, the study demonstrates that the chosen
p_{\text{T}}
acceptance for defining the normalized spectrum introduces a horizontal shift along the
p_{\text{T}}
axis for the zero-crossing point of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
. These results explains the influence of event classification schemes, spectral normalization procedures, and experimental acceptance on
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
to better interpret its features in experimental measurements and further assist probing the genuine collective dynamics in heavy-ion collisions.

5.1.7
Outlook

The results presented in this chapter open up several exciting avenues for future research, leveraging
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
to gain deeper insights into the QGP. The observed decrease of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
at
p_{\text{T}}>3
–
4
GeV in central collisions, similar to trends seen in anisotropic flow, warrants further investigation. This region may mark a transition where the interplay between collective hydrodynamic flow and energy loss mechanisms for hard-scattered partons (jet quenching) becomes important, and studying
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
at higher
p_{\text{T}}
could offer new perspectives on this interplay.

Concurrently, while the two-subevent method with an
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
effectively suppresses short-range non-flow correlations, their potential contributions at higher
p_{\text{T}}
and in peripheral collisions necessitate continued scrutiny. Model studies, such as those employing HIJING, and advanced techniques for non-flow subtraction or more sophisticated modeling of non-flow sources could help to further isolate the collective signal.

Beyond these refinements, extending
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measurements to smaller collision systems (e.g., p+Pb, O+O), different heavy-ion species (e.g., Xe+Xe), and a range of collision energies is crucial. This would allow for systematic studies of the system-size and energy dependence of radial flow fluctuations, addressing fundamental questions about the onset and evolution of collectivity.

Finally, building on the current analysis demonstrating the long-range nature of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
by varying
\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}
, more differential studies of the decorrelation of
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
as a function of the pseudorapidity separation between the measured particle and the region defining
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
could provide insights into the longitudinal structure and dynamics of radial flow fluctuations, similar to rapidity decorrelation studies performed for anisotropic flow
[
144
,
145
]
.

5.2
Disentangling Initial State Fluctuations Using Radial Flow

The previous chapter established radial flow as a collective phenomenon arising from the hydrodynamic expansion of the QGP and demonstrated its sensitivity to initial-state fluctuations. This collective outward boost influences the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra of particle produced in the final-state. Specifically, the slope of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra is a direct reflection of the event-wise average transverse momentum,
[p_{\text{T}}]
.

Historically, the degree of radial flow in events has been inferred from fitting particle spectra using the blast-wave parametrization
[
4
,
22
,
29
]
. This approach is built on the physical picture of a thermally equilibrated source expanding with a collective velocity. In the blast-wave model, the final-state
p_{\text{T}}
of a particle is understood as a superposition of two components: a random, thermal motion characterized by the kinetic freeze-out temperature,
T_{\text{kin}}
, and an ordered, collective motion described by the radial flow velocity,
\beta
. In a simplified non-relativistic picture, a particle’s total energy is the sum of its thermal energy and the kinetic energy from the collective expansion,
E_{\text{tot}}=E_{\text{th}}(T_{\text{kin}})+\frac{1}{2}m\beta^{2}
. By simultaneously fitting this model to the measured
p_{T}
spectra of identified hadrons, these two parameters,
T_{\text{kin}}
and
\beta
, can be extracted, effectively constraining these parameters.

Figure
5.23
presents a historical overview of results from the STAR and ALICE collaborations, showing
T_{\text{kin}}
versus
\langle\beta\rangle
extracted from these ensemble-averaged fits for different centrality bins and particle species
[
4
,
22
,
29
]
. The plot illustrates a characteristic anticorrelation: more central collisions (e.g., 0–5%) exhibit higher
\langle\beta\rangle
and lower
T_{\text{kin}}
compared to peripheral collisions (e.g., 80–90%). However, these extracted parameters are ensemble averages and do not directly capture the event-by-event fluctuations in the system’s collective expansion. To probe the dynamical nature of radial flow fluctuations and their connection to the QGP properties, it is necessary to study the distribution of event-by-event fluctuations in
[p_{\text{T}}]
.

Figure 5.23
:
Kinetic freezeout temperature (
T_{\text{kin}}
) vs. average radial flow velocity (
\langle\beta\rangle
) extracted from blast-wave model fits to identified particle spectra by the ALICE and STAR collaborations for different centrality classes. The ellipses represent statistical and systematic uncertainties
[
4
,
22
,
29
]
.

5.2.1
Theoretical Background

The distribution of the
[p_{\text{T}}]
,
P([p_{\text{T}}])
, is proposed to be a sensitive probe of the initial-state geometry, its fluctuations, and the thermodynamic properties, including the equation of state (EoS) and the speed of sound squared (
c_{s}^{2}
), of the QGP
[
146
,
147
,
31
,
30
,
148
,
149
,
150
]
. The shape of the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution can be characterized by its moments: the mean
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, variance
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{2}\right\rangle
, and skewness
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{3}\right\rangle
, where
\delta p_{\text{T}}=[p_{\text{T}}]-\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
represents the event-by-event fluctuation of
[p_{\text{T}}]
around its ensemble average
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
.

In a simple independent superposition scenario, where nuclear collisions are viewed as a collection of independent particle production from participating nucleons followed by final-state interactions, both geometrical and intrinsic fluctuations are expected to scale with the charged particle multiplicity,
N_{\text{ch}}
, approximately as
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{2}\right\rangle\propto 1/N_{\text{ch}}
and
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{3}\right\rangle\propto 1/N_{\text{ch}}^{\;2}

[
151
,
152
]
. However, experimental measurements of
[p_{\text{T}}]
correlations have revealed fluctuations in excess of this independent superposition baseline, providing evidence for the existence of additional dynamical correlations in heavy-ion collisions
[
153
,
154
]
.

5.2.1.1
Sources of
[p_{\text{T}}]
Fluctuations

Drawing from the understanding that individual particle transverse momentum reflects both the system’s collective expansion and its thermal properties, the overall event-by-event fluctuation in average transverse momentum,
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
, can be considered to originate from broadly two distinct categories of effects:

•

Geometrical fluctuations:
Variations in the initial size and shape of the nuclear overlap region from event to event. The hydrodynamic response translates fluctuations in the transverse size
R
into fluctuations in the collective radial flow, approximated as
\delta p_{\text{T}}/\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle\approx-\delta R/\left\langle R\right\rangle

[
126
]
.

•

Intrinsic fluctuations:
Other stochastic sources at a fixed geometry, including fluctuations in the positions of nucleons and partons, initial energy deposition, and the temperature profile of the QGP fluid
[
31
]
.

Recent phenomenological studies suggest that these two components of fluctuations can be effectively investigated using moments of
P([p_{\text{T}}])
in ultra-central collisions (UCC)
[
155
,
31
,
30
]
as is described below.

5.2.1.2
Disentangling Components of
[p_{\text{T}}]
Fluctuations

Figure
5.24
shows a schematic of the two-dimensional Gaussian model, which describes two components of fluctuations along two axes: one representing variations in the impact parameter
b
and the other representing variations in charged-particle multiplicity
N_{\text{ch}}
. Within this model framework, at a fixed
b
, fluctuations in the initial energy or entropy lead to variations in
N_{\text{ch}}
, capturing the intrinsic component of the fluctuations. Conversely, at a fixed
N_{\text{ch}}
, event-by-event changes in
b
capture the geometric component of the fluctuations.

Figure 5.24
:
Conceptual illustration of the two-dimensional Gaussian model for
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations. This model is defined using two axes representing
b
and
N_{\text{ch}}
. Event-by-event variations in
b
and
N_{\text{ch}}
are described by the 2D-Gaussian distribution, and the
[p_{\text{T}}]
for each event is treated as a single-valued function of
b
and
N_{\text{ch}}
.

In UCC, as
b\to 0
, the transverse overlap radius
R
approaches its maximum. This inherently suppresses geometric fluctuations. Given that
\delta R
and
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
are anticorrelated, this suppression of
\delta R
truncates the lower tail of the
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
distribution. This effect is clearly depicted in Figure
5.25
, which shows
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
distributions for increasing
N_{\text{ch}}
in UCC from the 2D Gaussian model simulations
[
30
]
. For more central collision event selections by selecting on
N_{\text{ch}}
, the allowed range for fluctuations in
b
within the model framework gets further suppressed resulting in the
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
distribution being increasingly cut off from the left. This truncation leads to a sharp decrease in the variance and a corresponding increase in the skewness of the
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
distribution.

These pronounced changes in variance and skewness specifically arise from the constraint imposed on geometric fluctuations in UCC, while intrinsic fluctuations are largely unaffected. Therefore, measuring the moments of the
[p_{\text{T}}]
distribution in UCC offers a valuable experimental handle to disentangle contributions from geometric fluctuations from those arising from intrinsic sources.

Figure 5.25
:
Model calculations showing the distribution of
\delta[p_{\text{T}}]
for different values of charged‐particle multiplicity
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
. The solid green lines represent a simplified model, while the dashed red lines show a more complete calculation. The shape and skewness of these distributions are sensitive to the underlying fluctuation sources.

5.2.1.3
Constraining
c_{s}^{2}

In addition to constraining sources of initial-state fluctuations, the measurement of
\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle
in UCC is also suggested to be sensitive to the speed of sound,
c_{s}^{2}
, in the QGP. Thermodynamically,
c_{s}^{2}
is defined as the derivative of pressure,
P
, with respect to energy density,
\epsilon
:

c_{s}^{2}=\frac{dP}{d\epsilon}.

(5.17)

This can be related to derivatives involving temperature,
T
, and entropy density,
s
. It has been argued that the effective temperature of the system,
T_{\text{eff}}
, is approximately proportional to the average transverse momentum,
\langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle
, and that the entropy density,
s
, is approximately proportional to the charged-particle multiplicity,
N_{\text{ch}}

[
146
,
147
]
. These proportionalities imply the following approximate relations for their logarithmic derivatives:

\displaystyle\frac{dT_{\text{eff}}}{T_{\text{eff}}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{d\langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle}{\langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle}\quad\Rightarrow\quad d(\ln T_{\text{eff}})\approx d(\ln\langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle),

(5.18)

\displaystyle\frac{ds(T_{\text{eff}})}{s(T_{\text{eff}})}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{dN_{\text{ch}}}{N_{\text{ch}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad d(\ln s)\approx d(\ln N_{\text{ch}}).

Using these approximations, and the thermodynamic identity
c_{s}^{2}=d(\ln T)/d(\ln s)
(for a system at negligible chemical potential and fixed volume),
c_{s}^{2}
can be proposed as an experimentally accessible quantity:

c_{s}^{2}=\frac{d(\ln T)}{d(\ln s)}\approx\frac{d(\ln\langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle)}{d(\ln N_{\text{ch}})}.

(5.19)

In UCC, the transverse overlap area is approximately fixed, approximately satisfying the criteria of a fixed volume. Within this fixed volume, variations in the initial entropy is expected to lead to variations in
N_{\text{ch}}
. At the same time, a larger initial entropy density is expected to drive a stronger collective radial expansion, resulting in a larger
\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle
. Consequently, the slope of
\ln\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle
versus
\ln N_{\text{ch}}
in UCC, i.e.,
\frac{d(\ln\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle)}{d(\ln N_{\text{ch}})}
, is predicted to provide a direct experimental constraint on
c_{s}^{2}
.

5.2.2
Previous Studies

The mean
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
and variance
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{2}\right\rangle
of the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution have been measured across various collision energies and system sizes over the past two decades
[
128
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
,
160
,
153
,
161
,
162
,
154
,
163
]
. These studies consistently show an increase in
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
towards more central collisions and confirm that the variance approximately follows the expected
1/N_{\text{ch}}
power-law scaling predicted by the independent superposition scenario.

More recently, ALICE reported initial measurements of the skewness
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{3}\right\rangle
and kurtosis
\left\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{4}\right\rangle
in broad multiplicity ranges for Xe+Xe and Pb+Pb collisions
[
133
]
. A detailed study by CMS collaboration focused on the behavior of
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
in Pb+Pb UCC and attempted to extract
c_{s}^{2}

[
164
]
, although with noted caveats regarding the interpretation
[
148
,
149
,
150
,
165
]
.

A common limitation of these previous studies is that they could not disentangle the contributions of geometrical and intrinsic fluctuations to the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution and its moments. Achieving this disentanglement requires precise measurements of higher-order moments, in narrow centrality intervals, especially in UCC.

This chapter presents the measurement of the mean, variance, and skewness of
P([p_{\text{T}}])
as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
in
208
Pb+
208
Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=5.02
TeV and
129
Xe+
129
Xe collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=5.44
TeV. Comparing results from the smaller Xe+Xe system with Pb+Pb provides a unique opportunity to study the role of system size in the scaling behavior of these moments and the underlying fluctuation components.

5.2.3
Methodology

The moments of the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution are calculated using multiparticle correlation methods, similar to techniques developed for anisotropic flow analysis
[
100
,
32
]
. For a single event, the event-wise average transverse momentum and the unnormalized 2- and 3-particle correlators are computed as follows:

\displaystyle[p_{\text{T}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}p_{\mathrm{T},i}}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}

(5.20)

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}w_{i}w_{j}\delta p_{i}\delta p_{j}}{\sum_{i\neq j}w_{i}w_{j}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}\delta p_{i}\delta p_{j}\delta p_{k}}{\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}}

(5.22)

Here,
p_{\mathrm{T},i}
is the transverse momentum of track
i
,
\delta p_{i}\equiv p_{\mathrm{T},i}-\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
is the deviation of the track’s
p_{\text{T}}
from the ensemble-averaged mean transverse momentum for the given event class, and
w_{i}\equiv(1-f_{i})/\epsilon_{i}
are weights applied to track
i
to correct for reconstruction efficiency,
\epsilon_{i}
, and fake rate,
f_{i}

[
107
]
. The ensemble averages of these correlators, denoted by
\left\langle c_{2}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle c_{3}\right\rangle
, are then used to define dimensionless, normalized moments:

k_{2}=\frac{\left\langle c_{2}\right\rangle}{\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle^{2}},\quad k_{3}=\frac{\left\langle c_{3}\right\rangle}{\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle^{3}},\quad\gamma=\frac{\left\langle c_{3}\right\rangle}{\left\langle c_{2}\right\rangle^{3/2}},\quad\Gamma=\frac{\left\langle c_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle}{\left\langle c_{2}\right\rangle^{2}}.

(5.23)

In this context,
\gamma
is referred to as the “standard skewness” as it represents the skewness for a distribution with unit variance, while
\Gamma
is termed the “intensive skewness”
[
166
]
. Under the independent superposition scenario, these normalized moments are expected to scale approximately as
k_{2}\propto 1/N_{\text{ch}}
,
k_{3}\propto 1/(N_{\text{ch}})^{2}
, and
\gamma\propto 1/\sqrt{N_{\text{ch}}}
, while
\Gamma
should be roughly independent of
N_{\text{ch}}
. Further discussions on these formulae can be found in Appendix
6.7
.

Statistical uncertainties for these observables are determined using a Poisson bootstrap method
[
138
,
139
]
. Further description on the Bootstrap method used can be found in Appendix
6.11
. Systematic uncertainties arise from various experimental factors, including track selection criteria, reconstruction efficiency corrections, residual pileup effects, centrality definition, and potential biases assessed via Monte Carlo (MC) consistency checks.

Systematic uncertainties from track selection, evaluated by varying selection criteria, are generally small, below 0.5% for
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
and
k_{3}
, and ranging from 0.5% to 4% for
k_{2}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
. Efficiency uncertainties, primarily from detector material simulation in
Geant
, can be up to 4% for individual track efficiency
[
107
]
, propagating to uncertainties around 1% for
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, 0.5% for
k_{2}
, 2–2.5% for
k_{3}
, 1–1.5% for
\gamma
, and 1.5–2.5% for
\Gamma
. Azimuthal efficiency variations are accounted for via track weights and found to have negligible impact. Residual pileup contributes uncertainties less than 0.5% for all moments. Centrality definition uncertainties, assessed by varying Glauber model parameters, are relevant when binning results by centrality and are below 0.5% in UCC.

Consistency checks using HIJING MC samples involve calculating moments using truth particles and comparing them to results obtained from reconstructed tracks with the same analysis procedure and corrections applied to real data
[
125
,
37
]
1
1

1

While absolute
[p_{\text{T}}]
-moments in HIJING may differ from data, the MC check validates the analysis procedure’s ability to reproduce moments from reconstructed tracks relative to truth level particles discussed in Section
4.5.2
, accounting for effects like smearing of
[p_{\text{T}}]
and
N_{\text{ch}}
.
. These checks show differences less than 0.25% for
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
and
k_{2}
, and about 1.2% for
k_{3}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
.

Total systematic uncertainties are obtained by adding individual contributions in quadrature. For most observables, total systematic uncertainties are smaller than statistical uncertainties, except for
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
where they are comparable. The largest systematic contributions generally come from track selection. Across both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe systems, total systematic uncertainties are typically below 1% for
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, 2–4% for
k_{2}
, 2–5% for
k_{3}
, and 2–4% for
\gamma
and
\Gamma
. The uncertainty in the measured
N_{\text{ch}}
itself, mainly due to tracking efficiency and fake track corrections, reaches up to 3% in Pb+Pb UCC. More details on the contribution of different systematic sources can be found in Appendix
6.12
.

5.2.4
Results

5.2.4.1
Dependence on
N_{\text{ch}}

Figure 5.26
:
Panel (a) depicts the 2D distribution of
[p_{\text{T}}]
vs.
N_{\text{ch}}
in Pb+Pb collisions, with the solid and dashed lines indicating the mean and two standard deviations, respectively. Panels (b), (c), and (d) show the
N_{\text{ch}}
dependence of
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
,
k_{2}
, and
k_{3}
, respectively, for both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. Error bars represent statistical uncertainties and shaded boxes denote systematic uncertainties. Vertical dashed lines mark
N_{\text{ch}}
values at 5% centrality, 4230 for Pb+Pb, 2425 for Xe+Xe.

Figure
5.26
(a) shows the two-dimensional distribution of event-wise
[p_{\text{T}}]
as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
for Pb+Pb collisions. The solid line traces the mean of
[p_{\text{T}}]
at a given
N_{\text{ch}}
, while the dashed lines indicate the
\pm 2
standard deviation contours, illustrating the width of the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution. With increasing
N_{\text{ch}}
, a clear increase in the mean
[p_{\text{T}}]
and a decrease in the width of the distribution is observed. However, the width of the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
shown in this panel has statistical contributions from a limited number of tracks in an event, which is taken care of by using moment definitions.

The moments of
P([p_{\text{T}}])
are presented as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
for both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions in Figure
5.26
(b)-(d). The mean transverse momentum
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
increases with
N_{\text{ch}}
, with the rise being steeper in peripheral collisions and becoming milder towards mid-centrality. The normalized variance
k_{2}
and skewness
k_{3}
both exhibit a power-law-like decrease with increasing
N_{\text{ch}}
across most centrality ranges, broadly consistent with expectations from the independent superposition scenario. However, for UCCs, beyond
N_{\text{ch}}
values marking 5% centrality, all three observables show distinct deviations from these general trends:
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
displays a notable sharp increase, while
k_{2}
and
k_{3}
undergo a pronounced decrease.

5.2.4.2
Scaling Behavior and UCC Non-monotonicity

Figure 5.27
:
The
N_{\text{ch}}
dependence of (a)
N_{\text{ch}}k_{2}
, (b)
(N_{\text{ch}})^{2}k_{3}
, and (c)
\Gamma
in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. These quantities are normalized to highlight deviations from simple power-law scaling. Error bars show statistical uncertainties; shaded boxes represent systematic uncertainties. Vertical dashed lines mark
N_{\text{ch}}
values at 5% centrality.

To better visualize deviations from simple power-law scaling, Figure
5.27
presents
N_{\text{ch}}k_{2}
in panel (a),
(N_{\text{ch}})^{2}k_{3}
in panel (b), and
\Gamma
in panel (c) as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
. In both systems,
N_{\text{ch}}k_{2}
and
(N_{\text{ch}})^{2}k_{3}
show an initial increase up to
N_{\text{ch}}\approx 1500
, consistent with the development of radial flow
[
167
]
. Beyond this point, their variation becomes more gradual until the UCC region. The intensive skewness
\Gamma
exhibits a slight decrease from peripheral to mid-central collisions before becoming relatively flat until the UCC region.

Within the UCC, a pronounced non-monotonic behavior is observed in all these scaled quantities.
N_{\text{ch}}k_{2}
decreases, while
(N_{\text{ch}})^{2}k_{3}
and
\Gamma
show abrupt increases followed by sharp decreases. These non-monotonic features are interpreted as a consequence of the suppression of the initial nuclear overlap transverse size distribution,
P(R)
, as the impact parameter approaches zero and
R
reaches its maximum value
[
31
]
. This suppression at large
R
values initially introduces a positive skewness in the
P([p_{\text{T}}])
distribution, which then diminishes as the variance of
P(R)
is further reduced in the most central events.

5.2.4.3
Extraction of Geometrical and Intrinsic Components

Figure 5.28
:
The (a)
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, (b)
k_{2}
, (c)
k_{3}
, and (d)
\Gamma
, scaled by their respective values at 5% centrality, as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}/N_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{5\%}}
for Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. Data are compared to predictions from Ref.
[
30
]
and its estimated geometrical component (dashed lines). Error bars show statistical uncertainties; shaded boxes represent systematic uncertainties.

Figure 5.29
:
(a)
\gamma
as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
and (b) scaled-
\gamma
as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}/N_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{5\%}}
in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions compared to predictions from Ref.
[
30
]
. Error bars and shaded areas represent statistical and systematic uncertainties.

To highlight the non-monotonic trends in UCC and facilitate a direct comparison between collision systems, Figure
5.28
shows
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
,
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
, and
\Gamma
, each scaled by their values at 5% centrality, plotted against normalized multiplicity
N_{\text{ch}}/N_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{5\%}}
. Figure
5.29
presents the standard skewness
\gamma
and scaled-
\gamma
similarly.

Qualitatively similar trends are observed across all scaled observables in both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe systems. However, the magnitude of the variations in UCC is somewhat attenuated in the smaller Xe+Xe system. This difference is consistent with expectations, as the broader
N_{\text{ch}}
distribution in Xe+Xe (due to its smaller size) results in a less effective selection of events with minimal geometric fluctuations at the highest multiplicities compared to Pb+Pb. This underscores the importance of system-size comparisons for understanding the nature of these fluctuations.

These results are compared to a recent model that describes
P([p_{\text{T}}])
fluctuations using a 2D Gaussian distribution in
N_{\text{ch}}
and impact parameter, attributing fluctuations at fixed
N_{\text{ch}}
primarily to variations in impact parameter and transverse size
R

[
31
,
30
]
. Within this framework, the scaled observables are decomposed into geometrical and intrinsic components.

The model broadly reproduces the increasing trend of scaled
\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
and the decreasing trend of scaled
k_{2}
observed in Pb+Pb data. However, it predicts a steeper decrease for scaled
k_{3}
,
\Gamma
, and
\gamma
in Figures
5.28
and
5.29
, with increasing
N_{\text{ch}}/N_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{5\%}}
compared to the data. The larger measured values for the scaled skewness observables suggest the presence of additional sources of skewness in
P([p_{\text{T}}])
that are not fully captured by the current model implementation. The dashed lines in Figure
5.28
illustrate the estimated contribution from the geometrical component according to the model, indicating that the UCC variation in
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
,
\Gamma
and
\gamma
can be attributed to geometrical fluctuations. This comparison highlights the model’s potential for disentangling components while also pointing to areas for further theoretical refinement.

5.2.4.4
Constraining
c^{2}_{s}
using UCC

Figure 5.30
:
Correlation between
\Delta p_{\text{T}}/\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{0-1\%}
and
\Delta N_{\text{ch}}/\left\langle N_{\text{ch}}\right\rangle_{0-1\%}
in the 0–1% most central Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions for two
p_{\text{T}}
ranges. Error bars show statistical uncertainties; shaded boxes represent systematic uncertainties. Data are compared to the MUSIC hydrodynamic model (
c_{s}^{2}\approx 0.23
at
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 222
MeV) and HIJING model at
b=0
fm
[
31
]
.

The observed non-monotonic behavior of the skewness measures, notably their sharp ‘bump’-like shape in the ultra-central collisions, provides evidence for the suppression of geometric fluctuation sources. These geometric sources become constrained as
b\to 0
, allowing for enhanced sensitivity to intrinsic fluctuations, which may originate from quantum variations in the initial energy deposition profiles or from thermal fluctuations within the QGP medium at a nearly fixed collision geometry.

To specifically investigate the correlation between
[p_{\text{T}}]
and
N_{\text{ch}}
in the 0–1% most central events, we analyze
\Delta p_{\text{T}}/\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle_{0-1\%}
as a function of
\Delta N_{\text{ch}}/\left\langle N_{\text{ch}}\right\rangle_{0-1\%}
, where average values are taken over the 0–1% centrality bin. Figure
5.30
shows this correlation for two different
p_{\text{T}}
ranges for both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. A nearly linear relationship is observed in both systems, with consistent slopes between Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe. The improved consistency between the systems in this plot compared to Figure
5.28
is attributed to the more stringent centrality selection on both axes for normalization. The slope of this correlation, however, exhibits a dependence on the selected
p_{\text{T}}
range, indicating a kinematic sensitivity of the radial flow effects.

The data are compared to predictions from the HIJING model
[
104
]
, which lacks collective final-state interactions, and the state-of-the-art MUSIC hydrodynamic model
[
168
]
, which incorporates the full hydrodynamic evolution. HIJING underpredicts the measured slopes. In contrast, the MUSIC model successfully reproduces the slopes for Pb+Pb data across both
p_{\text{T}}
ranges. This strong agreement with a hydrodynamic model in UCC where geometrical fluctuations are minimized, suggests that these measured slopes are a direct manifestation of the QGP’s hydrodynamic response to fluctuations in energy density at a nearly fixed transverse size.

The measured slopes in Figure
5.30
provide an experimental estimate for
c_{s}^{2}(T_{\mathrm{eff}})
. The MUSIC model’s description of the Pb+Pb data is achieved with
c_{s}^{2}\approx 0.23
at an effective temperature
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 222
MeV, consistent with some previous estimations
[
164
]
. However, the extraction of
c_{s}^{2}
is known to be sensitive to various factors, including the kinematic selection criteria used for particles
[
148
,
150
]
. Crucially, since the slopes in Figure
5.30
are primarily driven by the intrinsic component of
P([p_{\text{T}}])
fluctuations in UCC, which is linked to the higher-order moments shown in Figure
5.28
, any model aiming for a quantitative extraction of
c_{s}^{2}
must simultaneously describe all these observables.

5.2.4.5
Caveats in extraction of
c^{2}_{s}

The extraction of the sound speed squared (
c_{s}^{2}
) from
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations is subject to several dependencies. Experimentally, the inferred
c_{s}^{2}
value can be sensitive to the event centrality definition (e.g., using charged particle multiplicity or forward energy) and the kinematic cuts applied to particles included in the
[p_{\text{T}}]
calculation, such as the lower kinematic cut on
p_{\text{T}}
of the particles
[
148
,
149
,
150
]
. An alternate method to calculate the dependence of the extracted
c^{2}_{s}
on the kinematic cuts on the
p_{\text{T}}
-range is further described in Appenidx
Appendix A: Kinematic Dependence of Extracted
c_{s}^{2}
.

Furthermore, the relationship between measured observables and
c_{s}^{2}
is inherently model-dependent, relying on the specific hydrodynamic framework used for interpretation. Different models or parameter choices can yield varying conclusions about the
c_{s}^{2}
value that best fits the data
[
148
,
149
,
150
]
. Moreover, the assumption that
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations solely probe the thermodynamic
c_{s}^{2}
may be an oversimplification.

5.2.5
Non-Collective Baseline from HIJING

5.2.5.1
Motivation

he HIJING model serves as a baseline for studying particle production and correlations in the absence of QGP-induced collective effects. This study
[
32
]
established a comprehensive framework for calculating
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations using HIJING simulations across various systems (
pp
,
p
+Pb, Xe+Xe, Pb+Pb) at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}\sim
5 TeV.

5.2.5.2
Methodology

The methodology involved calculating
p_{\text{T}}
cumulants as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}
. The formulae are described in details, previously in Eq.
5.23
and in Appendix
6.7
. Two methods were employed: the standard method considering all particles within
|\eta|<2.5
, and a two-subevent method dividing the event into rapidity-separated regions (e.g.,
-2.5<\eta<-0.75
and
0.75<\eta<2.5
). Comparing these methods allows for disentangling short-range correlations from longer-range ones.

5.2.5.3
Results

5.2.5.3.1
N_{\text{ch}}
Scaling of Cumulants

In large collision systems like Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe,
p_{\text{T}}
cumulants
\left\langle c_{n}\right\rangle
exhibit a clear power-law dependence on
N_{\text{ch}}
, approximately scaling as
\propto 1/N_{\text{ch}}^{(n-1)}
for
n=2,3,4
. This is consistent with the independent superposition picture inherent in HIJING, where
N_{\text{ch}}
serves as a proxy for the number of independent particle-producing sources.

To quantify the dependence of the cumulants on
N_{\text{ch}}
, they were fitted with a power-law expression of the form:
a/(N_{\text{ch}})^{b}
. Figure
5.31
illustrates this scaling, showing that the cumulants decrease in magnitude with increasing
N_{\text{ch}}
, well-described by the power-law fits. The fitted exponent
b
is close to
n-1
for
\left\langle c_{n}\right\rangle
supporting the understanding that the
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations within HIJING originate from a simple independent source scenario.

Figure 5.31
:
The
[p_{\text{T}}]
cumulants (without normalization) for particles in
0.2<p_{\text{T}}<2\text{ GeV}
vs
N_{\text{ch}}
in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. Solid lines show fits to
a/(N_{\text{ch}})^{b}

[
32
]
. Here,
\left\langle\left\langle p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
.

5.2.5.3.2  Subevent Analysis and Correlation Length

The two-subevent method is intended to suppress short-range correlations in multi-particle correlation measurements. Figure
5.32
compares the normalized cumulants
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
, and
k_{4}
obtained using the standard and two-subevent methods as a function of the charged-particle multiplicity,
N_{\text{ch}}
. The variance (
k_{2}
) calculated using the two-subevent method is approximately three times smaller than that obtained with the standard method in Pb+Pb collisions. This suppression indicates that short-range correlations, likely originating from jet fragmentation and resonance decays, predominantly contribute to the total
p_{T}
variance in the HIJING model.

In contrast, higher-order cumulants,
k_{3}
and
k_{4}
, exhibit progressively smaller differences between the two methods as the order of correlation increases. This trend aligns with the expectation that short-range non-flow correlations primarily influence correlations involving fewer particles, and their contributions diminish with an increasing number of correlated particles.

Figure 5.32
:
Variance (left), skewness (
k_{3}
, middle) and kurtosis (
k_{4}
, right)
p_{\text{T}}
cumulants in Pb+Pb using the standard (solid points) and two-subevent method (open points) for particles in
0.2<p_{\text{T}}<5.0\text{ GeV}
vs
N_{\text{ch}}

[
32
]
.

Furthermore, the dependence of the variance (
k_{2}
) on the rapidity separation (
\Delta\eta
) between particles is analyzed in Figure
5.33
. As
\Delta\eta
increases,
k_{2}
decreases and saturates for rapidity separations greater than approximately
0.5
units. This saturation implies that genuinely long-range correlations present in HIJING, which are mainly attributed to string dynamics, have a characteristic pseudorapidity correlation length of around
0.5
units.

Figure 5.33
:
(Left) Variance obtained for standard and two-subevent methods with various rapidity separations vs
N_{\text{ch}}
in Pb+Pb for
0.2<p_{\text{T}}<2\text{ GeV}
. (Right) Ratio of results from two-subevent method to standard method
[
32
]
.

5.2.5.3.3  System Size Dependence

Figure
5.34
compares cumulants across collision systems of varying sizes. The expectation from such a comparison is that fluctuations will decrease with increasing system size due to the larger number of contributing sources. It is observed that, although the average transverse momentum (
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
) increases with system size, the higher-order cumulants,
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
, and
k_{4}
, at comparable charged-particle multiplicities (
N_{\text{ch}}
) are larger in
pp
collisions compared to A+A collisions.

This behavior arises from different dominant sources of fluctuations: per-source fluctuations, such as those from minijets, play a significant role in high-multiplicity
pp
events. In contrast, fluctuations in the number of sources dominate in A+A collisions. Thus, distinct fluctuation behaviors are observed depending on the collision system type at a given multiplicity.

Figure 5.34
:
Comparison of
p_{\text{T}}
cumulants between different collision systems as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}

[
32
]
.

5.2.6
Conclusion

This chapter presented a comprehensive study of the event-by-event average transverse momentum distribution,
P([p_{\text{T}}])
, as a sensitive probe of initial-state geometry, its fluctuations, and the subsequent hydrodynamic evolution of the QGP. A key objective was to experimentally disentangle the contributions from geometrical and intrinsic fluctuations by measuring the mean, variance, and skewness of
P([p_{\text{T}}])
across a broad
N_{\text{ch}}
range in
208
Pb+
208
Pb and
129
Xe+
129
Xe collisions.

The variance and skewness are observed to follow an approximate power-law scaling with
N_{\text{ch}}
consistent with the independent superposition scenario in most centrality ranges, but marked deviations are observed in ultra-central collisions. These behaviors are consistent with the suppression of geometrical fluctuations as the overlap region reaches its maximum size near-zero impact parameter.

The HIJING study provides a quantitative baseline for
p_{\text{T}}
fluctuations in the absence of strong collective final-state effects. The observed
N_{\text{ch}}
scaling in A+A collisions supports an independent source picture. The subevent method reveals that short-range correlations dominate the
p_{\text{T}}
variance in HIJING, with a characteristic rapidity length scale around 0.5 units. System size comparisons highlight different fluctuation mechanisms in
pp
vs A+A collisions at high
N_{\text{ch}}
. Deviations of experimental data from these HIJING predictions can serve as signatures for collective phenomena and guide the development of more complete theoretical models.

The measured correlation between
\Delta[p_{\text{T}}]/\left\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
and
\Delta N_{\text{ch}}/\left\langle N_{\text{ch}}\right\rangle
in UCC is well-reproduced by state-of-the-art hydrodynamic models, suggesting that this correlation is a direct manifestation of the QGP’s hydrodynamic response to intrinsic fluctuations in UCC. The agreement is achieved with a
c_{s}^{2}\approx 0.23
at an effective temperature
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 222
MeV. The comparison between Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions revealed that the centrality dependence of the moments in UCC is slightly less pronounced in the smaller Xe+Xe system, highlighting the sensitivity of these measurements to system size.

In conclusion, the investigation of
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations provides a robust and complementary approach for constraining initial-state fluctuations and refining the understanding of the hydrodynamic response. The observed non-monotonic behavior of higher moments and the correlation between
[p_{\text{T}}]
and
N_{\text{ch}}
in UCC, offer powerful constraints for theoretical models aiming to describe the QGP EoS. Future work should focus on developing models capable of simultaneously reproducing all measured moments across the full centrality range and their kinematic dependencies for a more precise extraction of QGP properties like the speed of sound.

5.2.7
Outlook

The results presented in this chapter open several avenues for future research. Further experimental measurements of higher-order moments of the P(
[p_{\text{T}}]
) distribution with increased precision, esspecially in ultra-central collisions and across a wider range of system sizes and collision energies, will provide more stringent constraints for theoretical models. Detailed comparisons of these measurements with predictions from various hydrodynamic models, employing different initial-state descriptions and equations of state, are essential for a more robust extraction of QGP properties, including the speed of sound. Ultimately, the continued investigation of event-by-event fluctuations in observables like
[p_{\text{T}}]
promises to refine understanding of the initial conditions of heavy-ion collisions and the fundamental properties of the Quark-Gluon Plasma.

5.3
Constraining Nuclear Structure Using Elliptic and Radial Flow Correlation

Building upon the established collective nature of radial flow fluctuations discussed in Chapter
5.1
and their sensitivity to initial geometrical fluctuations discussed in Chapter
5.2
, this chapter focuses on leveraging multi-particle correlations between anisotropic flow (
v_{n}
) and the event-wise mean transverse momentum (
[p_{\text{T}}]
) in the final state to constrain the geometry of colliding nuclei and initial-state conditions.

The initial-state correlations between eccentricity,
\varepsilon_{n}^{2}
, and the inverse of the system size,
1/R_{\perp}
, survive the collective expansion of the system and, as a collective response, leave their imprints on final-state
v_{n}
-
[p_{\text{T}}]
correlations. As will be discussed later in this chapter, these
v_{n}
-
[p_{\text{T}}]
correlations are highly sensitive to: (1) Initial-state conditions, such as the nucleon width, and (2) Initial momentum anisotropy. Thus, experimental measurement of
v_{n}
-
[p_{\text{T}}]
correlations can provide strong constraints on the initial conditions of HIC.

Beyond initial conditions, relativistic HIC also offers a unique femtoscopic lens onto the structure of the colliding nuclei themselves
[
169
]
. This chapter leverages this potential by employing event-by-event correlations between
v_{n}
and
[p_{\text{T}}]
, as a sensitive probe of nuclear deformation parameters. By comparing such correlations in collisions of approximately spherical
{}^{208}\text{Pb}
nuclei with those involving
{}^{129}\text{Xe}
, this work aims to extract quantitative information about the shape of
{}^{129}\text{Xe}
. Of particular interest is the constraint on its quadrupole deformation parameter
\beta_{2}
and, notably, its triaxiality parameter
\gamma_{Xe}
, the latter being especially challenging to determine for odd-mass nuclei via traditional low-energy spectroscopic methods.

5.3.1
Theoretical Background

5.3.1.1
Nuclear Structure

Atomic nuclei are not always spherical
[
170
,
171
]
. Nuclei with partially filled nucleon shells often deviate from a spherical shape due to intrinsic quadrupole moment. The low-lying energy structure of such deformed nuclei can be described by collective rotational bands, where energy levels follow a rotational pattern:

E(I)\approx\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2\mathcal{I}}I(I+1),

where
I
represents the angular momentum and
\mathcal{I}
the nuclear moment of inertia. This pattern is particularly pronounced in even–even nuclei, where rotational levels typically appear with
I=0,2,4,\dots
, corresponding to collective rotational excitations. A key experimental indicator of this behavior is the ratio of the energy of the first
4^{+}
excited state to that of the first
2^{+}
state,
E(4^{+})/E(2^{+})
. For an ideal rigid rotor, this ratio is
10/3\approx 3.33
, considerably higher than the value of approximately
2.0
expected for vibrational excitations in spherical nuclei.

Figure 5.35
:
(Left) Typical energy level schemes for rotational and vibrational states. (Right) The ratio
E(4^{+})/E(2^{+})
vs. the mass number of nuclei. Red circles denote nuclei with a high
E(4^{+})/E(2^{+})
ratio, indicating strong rotational behavior and deformation.

The degree of non-sphericity is quantified by the quadrupole deformation parameter,
\beta
, which is directly related to the intrinsic quadrupole moment
Q_{0}
, a measure of the nuclear density distribution. For an axially symmetric nucleus, the nuclear radius
R
as a function of the angle
\theta^{\prime}
(relative to the symmetry axis) can be expressed using spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell m}

[
35
]
:

R(\theta^{\prime})=R_{0}\bigl(1+\beta\,Y_{20}(\theta^{\prime})\bigr).

(5.24)

A positive
\beta
indicates a prolate (football-like) deformation, while a negative value signifies an oblate (disc-like) deformation. This inherent deformation, probed through energy level structures and spectroscopic signatures, is important for understanding nuclear structure and influences high-energy nuclear collisions by affecting the initial geometric anisotropies.

In collisions involving deformed nuclei, fluctuations in the overlap area size and eccentricity are considerably larger than in collisions of spherical nuclei. Consider, for instance, a prolately deformed nucleus. While a central collision (
b=0
) of spherical nuclei results in a circular overlap, the overlap characteristics for a prolately deformed nucleus vary with its orientation. A “tip–tip” configuration (collision along the deformation axis) yields a smaller, more circular overlap compared to spherical nuclei, leading to a smaller effective overlap radius
R_{\mathrm{overlap}}
and ellipticity
\varepsilon_{2}
. Conversely, a “body–body” configuration (collision perpendicular to the deformation axis) results in a larger overlap area, yielding increased
R_{\mathrm{overlap}}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
compared to a central collision of spherical nuclei, as depicted in Figure
5.36
.

Figure 5.36
:
Cartoon of two extreme orientations for collisions of two prolately deformed nuclei.
\varepsilon_{2}
is ellipticity whereas
d_{\perp}=1/R_{\perp}
is the inverse transverse size.

5.3.1.2
Effect of Nuclear Deformation on
\rho(v^{2}_{2},[p_{\text{T}}])

The geometry of collisions involving deformed nuclei is orientation-dependent, leading to specific correlations that are not typically observed in collisions of spherical nuclei. For a given centrality, a “tip–tip” orientation (resulting in a smaller, rounder overlap) leads to a smaller initial eccentricity
\varepsilon_{2}
and consequently a smaller anisotropic flow
v_{2}
. However, this configuration also results in a larger event-wise average transverse momentum,
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
, due to stronger pressure gradients arising from the smaller system size.

Conversely, a “body–body” orientation (resulting in a larger, more elongated overlap) leads to a larger
\varepsilon_{2}
and thus a larger
v_{2}
, but a smaller
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
. This interplay between the collision geometry and the resulting flow and momentum is expected to generate a negative correlation between
v_{2}
(or
v_{2}^{2}
) and
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
in the final state for such deformed systems.

To quantify these correlations, the Pearson correlation coefficient,
\rho_{n}
, has been proposed
[
33
]
:

\rho_{n}=\frac{\bigl\langle\bigl\langle v_{n}^{2}\,\delta p_{\text{T}}\bigr\rangle\bigr\rangle}{\sqrt{\bigl(\langle v_{n}^{4}\rangle-\langle v_{n}^{2}\rangle^{2}\bigr)}\,\sqrt{\bigl\langle\langle(\delta p_{\text{T}})^{2}\rangle\bigr\rangle}},

(5.25)

where
\delta p_{T}=p_{T}-\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\rm evt}
and
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\rm evt}
is the average transverse momentum of particles in a given event. The notation
\langle\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
denotes averaging over all particle pairs (or triplets, depending on the specific observable definition) within events of comparable particle multiplicity, while
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes averaging over all such events.

Initial calculations for collisions of spherical nuclei, such as Pb+Pb, predicted that
\rho_{n}
(specifically for
n=2
) would be large and positive in mid-central collisions, approaching zero in peripheral collisions, as shown in Figure
5.37
. This positive correlation was attributed to overall system size fluctuations linked to centrality variations.

Figure 5.37
:
v_{2}
-
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
correlation (denoted
\rho_{2}
) for Pb+Pb collisions. The stars denote the Pearson coefficient, the circles denote the correlation coefficient without self-correlations, and the triangles denote calculations with oversampled events
[
33
]
.

Recent studies have further highlighted the sensitivity of
\rho_{n}
in central collisions to the detailed shape of atomic nuclei, including triaxiality
[
172
,
19
,
173
,
174
,
45
,
35
]
. Most nuclei exhibit some degree of deformation into an ellipsoidal shape, which can be described by the nuclear surface equation
[
171
]
:

R(\theta,\phi)=R_{0}\Bigl(1+\beta\bigl[\cos\gamma\,Y_{2,0}(\theta,\phi)+\tfrac{\sin\gamma}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl(Y_{2,2}(\theta,\phi)+Y_{2,-2}(\theta,\phi)\bigr)\bigr]\Bigr),

(5.26)

where
R_{0}
is the average nuclear radius,
Y_{\ell m}
are spherical harmonics, and
\beta
and
\gamma
are the quadrupole deformation parameters. The parameter
\beta
(often denoted
\beta_{2}
for quadrupole deformation) quantifies the magnitude of the deformation (typically 0.1–0.4
[
170
]
), while the angle
\gamma
(ranging from
0^{\circ}
to
60^{\circ}
) describes the triaxiality, representing the relative lengths of the three semi-axes (
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}
) of the ellipsoid. Specifically,
\gamma=0^{\circ}
corresponds to a prolate shape (
r_{1}=r_{2}<r_{3}
, with
r_{3}
as the symmetry axis),
\gamma=60^{\circ}
to an oblate shape (
r_{1}<r_{2}=r_{3}
, with
r_{1}
as the symmetry axis), and intermediate values like
\gamma=30^{\circ}
to a maximally triaxial shape (
2r_{2}\approx r_{1}+r_{3}
).

Traditionally, nuclear shapes are inferred from low-energy spectroscopic measurements, which determine
(\beta,\gamma)
for even–even nuclei (e.g., deformed isotopes of Samarium or Uranium, while
208
Pb is spherical)
[
175
]
. The shapes of odd-mass nuclei, such as
129
Xe, are typically calculated using nuclear structure models tuned to data from even–even nuclei. Therefore, flow measurements in high-energy HIC offer a novel tool to probe nuclear shapes, particularly for odd-mass nuclei.

Furthermore, both the magnitude of quadrupole deformation
\beta_{2}
and the triaxiality parameter
\gamma
are expected to influence
\rho_{2}
(the correlation coefficient for
n=2
). This provides a valuable means to constrain even these higher-order deformations in nuclei like
129
Xe, which are otherwise challenging to extract using traditional spectroscopic methods. Figure
5.38
presents model studies demonstrating the sensitivity of
\rho_{2}
to
\beta_{2}
and
\gamma
. The left panel shows that increasing
\beta_{2}
of the colliding deformed nucleus leads to an increase in
\rho_{2}
, consistent with the parametric form discussed later in the text. The right panel illustrates that transitioning from a prolately deformed nucleus (
\gamma<30^{\circ}
) to an oblate one (
\gamma>30^{\circ}
) decreases
\rho_{2}
in the most central collisions.

Figure 5.38
:
(Left) Influence of increasing quadrupole deformation
\beta_{2}
on
\rho_{2}

[
34
]
. (Right) Influence of triaxiality
\gamma
on
\rho_{2}

[
35
]
in model simulated U+U collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}
=200 GeV.

Therefore, precise measurements of
\rho_{n}
are crucial for constraining:

•

The nuclear structure of deformed nuclei, including odd-mass nuclei like
129
Xe.

•

Refining initial conditions for HIC models, such as the nucleon width.

•

Addressing the long-standing question of experimental evidence for initial momentum anisotropy.

Recent model studies suggest that
v_{2}^{2}
and
\rho_{2}
follow simple parametric forms
[
176
,
173
,
35
]
:

v_{2}^{2}\approx a+b\,\beta^{2},\quad\rho_{2}\approx a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}\,\cos(3\gamma)\,\beta^{3}.

(5.27)

The parameters
a
and
a^{\prime}
represent the baseline values for collisions of spherical nuclei (
\beta=0
) and are smallest in central collisions. The parameters
b
and
b^{\prime}
are nearly independent of centrality. Consequently, the impact of nuclear deformation (characterized by
\beta
and
\gamma
) on
v_{2}^{2}
and
\rho_{2}
is expected to be most pronounced in central collisions. This is further illustrated by simulations of deformed U+U collisions using a Glauber model, as shown in Figure
5.39
. These simulations demonstrate that as the quadrupole deformation
\beta
(or
\beta_{2}
) increases,
\langle\varepsilon_{2}^{2}\rangle
clearly increases, with the largest increase observed in the most central collisions.

The middle panel of Figure
5.39
shows that the excess
\varepsilon_{2}^{2}
relative to the spherical baseline,
\varepsilon_{2}^{2}(0)
, is linearly correlated with
\beta_{2}^{2}
, consistent with Eq.
5.27
. Lastly, the extracted coefficient
b^{\prime}
for
n=2
as a function of centrality shows a slow rise towards the most central collisions. This indicates that nuclear deformation is expected to maximally influence the measured flow coefficients and their correlations in the most central collisions, providing an experimental avenue for its extraction.

Figure 5.39
:
(Left)
\langle\varepsilon_{2}^{2}\rangle
in collisions of U+U nuclei with different quadrupole deformation parameters
\beta_{2}
. (Center) Excess
\langle\varepsilon_{2}^{2}\rangle
with respect to the spherical baseline,
\langle\varepsilon_{2}^{2}(0)\rangle
, vs.
\beta_{2}^{2}
, showing linear correlation and validating the form in Eq.
5.27
for
v_{2}^{2}
. (Right) Value of extracted coefficient
b_{2}^{\prime}
vs. centrality for the same system, using a form similar to Eq.
5.27
for
\rho_{2}
.

5.3.1.3
Effect of initial conditions

Subsequent model investigations have revealed the sensitivity of
\rho_{n}
to several factors beyond just system size and deformation. In central and mid-central collisions,
\rho_{n}
is generally expected to be positive due to centrality fluctuations: larger systems (corresponding to higher centrality, i.e., more participating nucleons) tend to exhibit both larger
v_{n}
and larger
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}

[
24
]
.

Furthermore, the presence of initial momentum anisotropy can cause
\rho_{n}
to become positive in the most peripheral collisions, as illustrated in Figure
5.40

[
23
]
. The root-mean-square size of the nucleon also influences the behavior of
\rho_{n}
in peripheral collisions; for instance, a larger nucleon size tends to decrease the value of
\rho_{n}
, as shown in Figure
5.41

[
36
]
.

Figure 5.40
:
Effect of initial momentum anisotropy on
v_{2}
-
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
correlations within IP-Glasma+MUSIC+URQMD model simulations for Pb+Pb collisions at 5.02 TeV and Au+Au collisions at 200 GeV
[
23
]
.

Figure 5.41
:
v_{2}
-
\langle p_{T}\rangle_{\text{evt}}
correlation for Pb+Pb collisions in the TRENTo model with varying nucleon width parameter,
w
, for smooth nucleons and nucleons with 3 hotspots with constituent quark width
w_{q}

[
36
]
.

5.3.2
Previous Studies

A quadrupole deformation for
129
Xe,
\beta_{2,\rm Xe}\approx 0.16\!-\!0.2
, was inferred from the enhanced ratio
v_{2,\rm Xe}/v_{2,\rm Pb}
observed in central collisions by both ALICE and ATLAS
[
38
,
37
]
. Figure
5.42
illustrates that incorporating this large
\beta_{2,\rm Xe}
in HIC models is essential for describing the
v_{2}
ratio between deformed
129
Xe and spherical
208
Pb nuclei.

Figure 5.42
:
v_{2}\{2\}
ratio between Xe+Xe collisions at 5.44 TeV and Pb+Pb collisions at 5.02 TeV as a function of centrality from ATLAS (Left) and ALICE (Right) collaborations
[
37
,
38
]
. While the ATLAS measurement is compared to Trento model calculations
[
39
]
, the ALICE measurement is compared to IP-Glasma+MUSIC+URQMD model calculations
[
40
]
.

ATLAS previously published a measurement of the
v_{n}
-
[p_{\text{T}}]
correlation coefficient,
\rho_{n}
, for
n=2,3,
and 4 in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5.02
TeV
[
107
]
. This was followed by similar measurements from ALICE in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions
[
177
]
. These results generally show positive correlations for all harmonics, except in the peripheral region where
\rho_{2}
becomes negative. These behaviors have been qualitatively reproduced by recent initial state and hydrodynamic model calculations
[
24
,
44
]
.

This chapter details a study of the centrality and system-size dependence of
\rho_{n}
in
{}^{129}\mathrm{Xe}+^{129}\mathrm{Xe}
and
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}+^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
collisions. The goal is to elucidate the effects of initial-state conditions and nuclear deformation. Measurements are performed across several ranges of transverse momentum (
p_{T}
) and pseudorapidity (
\eta
) to quantify the influence of final-state effects
[
24
]
. The
\rho_{n}
values are also affected by nonflow contributions, primarily from resonance decays and jets. These contributions can be mitigated using the ”subevent method,” as elucidated in Section
2.1.1
.

5.3.3
Methodology

In the experimental analysis, the measurement of
\rho_{n}
using Eq.
5.25
requires the calculation of its constituent terms. For this purpose, we define:

\displaystyle\rho_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])}{\sqrt{\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})}\sqrt{c_{k}}},\quad\text{where}

(5.28)

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle v_{n}^{2}\,\delta p_{\text{T}}\rangle\!\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})

\displaystyle=\left\langle v_{n}^{4}\right\rangle-\left\langle v_{n}^{2}\right\rangle^{2},

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\delta p_{\text{T}}\,\delta p_{\text{T}}\rangle\!\rangle.

The covariance
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
is a three-particle correlator, obtained by first calculating the average product for unique particle triplets within each event and subsequently averaging this quantity over all events in a specified event-activity class based on the number of reconstructed charged particles,
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
, and the total forward transverse energy,
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{\sum\nolimits_{i,j,k\,\atop i\neq j\neq k}w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}\,e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}\bigl(p_{T,k}-[p_{\text{T}}]\bigr)}{\sum\nolimits_{i,j,k\,\atop i\neq j\neq k}w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}}\right\rangle.

(5.29)

In this formula, the indices
i,j,k
iterate over distinct charged particles to include all unique triplets. The particle weight
w_{i}
, detailed in Section
4
, corrects for detector non-uniformities and tracking inefficiencies. The expression for
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
can be expanded algebraically within the cumulant framework
[
100
,
135
,
101
,
102
]
into a polynomial function of flow vectors and momentum-scalar quantities. These are constructed from event-wise sums:

\displaystyle\textbf{q}_{n;k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}e^{in\phi_{i}}}{\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}},\quad p_{m;k}=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}\bigl(p_{T,i}-[p_{\text{T}}]\bigr)^{m}}{\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}},\quad[p_{\text{T}}]=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}\,p_{T,i}}{\sum_{i}w_{i}},

(5.30)

where
k
and
m
are integer powers. Further details of this expansion are available in Ref.
[
41
]
.

The statistical uncertainty of the measurements is determined using a standard Poisson bootstrap method
[
138
,
139
]
. A further description of the Bootstrap method used can be found in Appendix
6.11
.

To compute the Pearson coefficient in Eq.
5.28
, the variances
c_{k}
and
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
must also be calculated. The term
c_{k}
represents the event-averaged two-particle
p_{T}
covariance:

c_{k}=\left\langle\frac{\sum\nolimits_{i,j\,\atop i\neq j}w_{i}w_{j}\,(p_{T,i}-[p_{\text{T}}])\,(p_{T,j}-[p_{\text{T}}])}{\sum\nolimits_{i,j\,\atop i\neq j}w_{i}w_{j}}\right\rangle.

This is obtained using all distinct particle pairs in the full event (i.e., within
|\eta|<2.5
).

The term
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
(the variance of
v_{n}^{2}
) is calculated using a hybrid approach that combines cumulants from different methods to balance nonflow reduction and statistical precision:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}

\displaystyle=c_{n}\{4\}_{\mathrm{standard}}+c_{n}\{2\}^{2}_{\mathrm{two-subevent}}

(5.31)

Here,
c_{n}\{2\}\equiv\left\langle v_{n}^{2}\right\rangle
is the two-particle cumulant and
c_{n}\{4\}\equiv\left\langle v_{n}^{4}\right\rangle-2(\left\langle v_{n}^{2}\right\rangle)^{2}
is the four-particle cumulant
[
178
]
; these are described further in an Appendix.

The
c_{n}\{4\}
term, being relatively insensitive to nonflow correlations but having poorer statistical precision
[
100
]
, is obtained from the standard method using the full event. The
c_{n}\{2\}
term, more susceptible to nonflow but with better precision, is calculated using the two-subevent method. The calculation of
c_{n}\{2\}
and
c_{n}\{4\}
follows established procedures
[
100
,
101
]
, expressing them in terms of the flow vectors
\textbf{q}_{n;k}
(defined in Eq.
5.30
). Further discussions on these formulas can be found in Appendix
6.7
.

Charged particles in this analysis are selected from predefined
p_{T}
ranges listed in Table
5.2
. For Pb+Pb data, two
p_{T}
ranges are used:
0.5<p_{T}<5\,
GeV and
0.5<p_{T}<2\,
GeV. For Xe+Xe data, an additional range,
0.3<p_{T}<2\,
GeV, is included. The primary results are based on the
0.5<p_{T}<5\,
GeV range due to its superior statistical precision.

Table 5.2
:
The
\eta
and
p_{T}
ranges chosen for the standard and subevent methods.

Method

Default
\eta
selection

Alternative
\eta
selection

|\eta|<2.5

,

,
0.35<\eta_{c}<1

-2.5<\eta_{a}<-0.75
,
|\eta_{b}|<0.5
,

,
|\eta_{b}|<0.3
,

selection for Xe+Xe

selection for Pb+Pb

GeV,

0.5<p_{T}<5\,
GeV,
0.5<p_{T}<2\,
GeV

The measurement of
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
,
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
, and
c_{k}
follows a three-step procedure
[
179
,
107
]
. First, these correlators are calculated event-wise. Second, their values are averaged over events with comparable multiplicity (e.g., similar
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
or the same
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
bin) and then combined in broader multiplicity ranges. The Pearson coefficients
\rho_{n}
are then obtained using Eq.
5.28
. This averaging procedure helps to reduce centrality fluctuation effects
[
180
,
24
,
181
]
. Third, the
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
dependence is converted to a centrality percentile dependence for comparability with other results.

Particle weights
w_{i}
, accounting for detector inefficiencies
\epsilon(\eta,p_{T})
and azimuthal non-uniformities, are applied. Residual
\phi
-dependencies in flow vectors
\textbf{q}_{n;k}
are corrected by subtracting an event-averaged offset, as discussed in Section
6.9
.

Comparison and Selection of Subevent Methods

To suppress short‐range nonflow contributions (e.g., from resonance decays and jets), an
\eta
gap is applied between particles, and three primary methods for calculating the correlators are compared
[
101
,
41
]
:

•

Standard method:
All charged particles with
|\eta|<2.5
are used.

•

Two‐subevent method:
Particles are taken from two separated subevents,
a\>(-2.5<\eta_{a}<-0.75)
and
c\>(0.75<\eta_{c}<2.5)
. For
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, one particle each contributing to
v_{n}^{2}
(one from
a
, one from
c
) is used, and the third particle (for the
p_{T}
term) is drawn from either subevent
a
or
c
, ensuring it is distinct from the first two.

•

Three‐subevent method:
Particles are taken from three subevents:
a\>(-2.5<\eta_{a}<-0.75)
,
b\>(|\eta_{b}|<0.5)
, and
c\>(0.75<\eta_{c}<2.5)
. For
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, the particles contributing to
v_{n}^{2}
are from subevents
a
and
c
, and the particle for the
p_{T}
term is from subevent
b
.

•

Combined-subevent method:
The three-particle correlators (like
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
) are calculated by averaging the results obtained from triplets formed using both the two-subevent and three-subevent configurations.

Table
5.2
lists the
\eta
and
p_{T}
selections used in this analysis, which are relevant for studying non-flow and longitudinal effects, along with the corresponding results presented in the following section. Furthermore, the justification for using certain subevent methods for specific results is provided in detail in Appendix
6.8
.

5.3.4
Results

The values of the constituent correlators
c_{k}
,
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
(variance of
v_{n}^{2}
), and
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
(covariance term) are calculated and combined to obtain the Pearson correlation coefficient
\rho_{n}
for each choice of
p_{T}
and
\eta
ranges in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions, as defined in Table
5.2
. In each case, these quantities can be obtained with the event-averaging procedure performed in intervals of either
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
or
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
; these intervals are then translated to average centrality values. The default event-averaging procedure is based on
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
.

As described in Section
5.3.3
, the primary results shown are calculated for charged particles in the range
0.5<p_{T}<5
GeV, using the three-subevent method for
\rho_{2}
and the combined-subevent method for
\rho_{3}
and
\rho_{4}
.

5.3.4.1
Dependence on method and collision systems

Figure 5.43
:
The centrality dependence of
\rho_{n}
for
n=
2 (left), 3 (middle), and 4 (right) in Pb+Pb (top) and Xe+Xe (bottom) collisions, calculated for the standard, two-subevent, and three-subevent methods. They are calculated using the event-averaging procedure based on
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
. The error bars and shaded boxes represent statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively. To reduce statistical fluctuations in the Xe+Xe data, wider centrality binning is used in the bottom row. The Pb+Pb
\rho_{2}
data are also compared with HIJING calculations from Ref.
[
41
]
, which include only nonflow correlations.

Figure
5.43
shows the
\rho_{n}
values obtained from the standard, two-subevent, and three-subevent methods for charged particles with
0.5<p_{T}<5
GeV in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions. These are obtained using the event-averaging procedure based on
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
and plotted as a function of centrality.

The results from the different methods are close to each other in central and mid-central collisions. In peripheral collisions beyond 60% centrality, the values from the standard method are larger than those obtained from the subevent methods. This is consistent with the presence of nonflow correlations arising from resonance decays and jets, which typically give positive contributions to both
v_{n}
and
[p_{\text{T}}]
in the standard method, thereby enhancing the correlation.

The nonflow effects in the two-subevent method, reflected by the difference from the three-subevent method, are also visible beyond 70% centrality. Smaller differences, albeit weakly dependent on centrality, are also observed between the two-subevent method and the three-subevent method in mid-central and central collisions. These differences are expected because the
v_{n}
signal, as well as the decorrelations of
v_{n}
and
[p_{\text{T}}]
, depend on the chosen
\eta
intervals and the
\eta
-gap (
\Delta\eta
), which differ between the two methods
[
145
,
182
,
33
]
.

The influence of nonflow effects was investigated recently in models
[
41
,
183
]
. The
\rho_{2}
values obtained from the HIJING model, which generates only nonflow correlations, show a similar ordering between the three methods, as seen in Figure
5.43
. In particular, the values of
\rho_{2}
from the three-subevent method are closer to zero in the multiplicity range corresponding to the centrality range shown. The non-zero
\rho_{2}
signal observed in the data in the peripheral region cannot be reproduced by the HIJING model, indicating contributions beyond these nonflow correlations.

The results from the subevent methods show similar centrality dependencies between the Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe systems: the
\rho_{2}
values reach a minimum in peripheral collisions, increase to a positive maximum value, and then decrease in the most central collisions; the
\rho_{3}
values show a mild increase towards central collisions; the
\rho_{4}
values show an increase then a gradual decrease towards central collisions.

In the ultracentral collision region, all the
\rho_{n}
values show a sharp decrease towards the most central collisions. This decrease is much clearer in the Pb+Pb system due to its better statistical precision and better centrality resolution than in the Xe+Xe system. This sharp decrease starts at around 1.6% centrality in Pb+Pb, which corresponds approximately to the location of the ”knee” in the minimum-bias
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
distribution
[
184
]
. For events having
\Sigma E_{\mathrm{T}}
