\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}}

(6.43)

First lets expand the denominator :

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}-3\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{3}

\displaystyle=S_{31}-3[\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{3}]-S_{13}

\displaystyle=S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+3S_{13}-S_{13}

\displaystyle=S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}

Next lets expand the numerator :

\displaystyle N=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{h}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{3}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|Q_{n1}|^{2}P_{11}-|Q_{n1}|^{2}S_{11}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle-\{\sum_{i}\sum_{h}w_{i}^{2}w_{h}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{3}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)\}

\displaystyle-2\{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{3}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)\}-P_{13}+S_{13}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|Q_{n1}|^{2}P_{11}-|Q_{n1}|^{2}S_{11}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle-S_{12}P_{11}+S_{12}S_{11}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle+P_{13}-S_{13}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle-2\Re(O_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2\Re(Q_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle+2P_{13}-2S_{13}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle-P_{13}+S_{13}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|Q_{n1}|^{2}P_{11}-S_{12}P_{11}-2\Re(O_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2P_{13}

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\{|Q_{n1}|^{2}S_{11}-S_{12}S_{11}-2\Re(Q_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2S_{13}\}

So the covariance for the standard method becomes :

\displaystyle Cov[FSE]=

\displaystyle\frac{|Q_{n1}|^{2}P_{11}-S_{12}P_{11}-2\Re(O_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2P_{13}}{S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}}

(6.44)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{|Q_{n1}|^{2}S_{11}-S_{12}S_{11}-2\Re(Q_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2S_{13}}{S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}}

(6.45)

or in terms of
\bar{p}
:

\displaystyle Cov[FSE]=

\displaystyle\frac{|Q_{n1}|^{2}\bar{P}_{11}-S_{12}\bar{P}_{11}-2\Re(\bar{O}_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2\bar{P}_{13}}{S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}}

(6.46)

6.7.3.1.2  Two-subevent

The three particle correlation of the
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
is given as :

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}}

(6.47)

In 2-subevent case the particle i and j belong to two different subevents - A and C and are never the same.

The expansion of denominator :

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}

The expansion of numerator :

\displaystyle N=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}^{2}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{11}-\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{11}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle-\Re(O_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(Q_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})

\displaystyle-\Re(Q_{An1}O_{Cn2}^{*})+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn2}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{11}-\Re(O_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}O_{Cn2}^{*})

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{11}-\Re(Q_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn2}^{*})\}

So the covariance for the 2-subevent becomes :

\displaystyle Cov[2SE]=

\displaystyle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{11}-\Re(O_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}O_{Cn2}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}}

(6.48)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{11}-\Re(Q_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn2}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}}

(6.49)

or in terms of
\bar{p}
:

\displaystyle Cov[2SE]=

\displaystyle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})\bar{P}_{11}-\Re(\bar{O}_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}\bar{O}_{Cn2}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}}

(6.50)

6.7.3.1.3  Three-subevent

The three particle correlation of the
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
is given as :

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}}

(6.51)

In three-subevent case the particle i and j belong to two different subevents - A and C and particle k is from the subevent B. The i,j and k particle are never the same.

The expansion of denominator :

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}

\displaystyle=S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{B11}

The expansion of numerator :

\displaystyle N=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}(p_{h}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{B11}-\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{B11}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle

So the covariance for the three-subevent becomes :

\displaystyle Cov[3SE]=

\displaystyle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{B11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{B11}}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{B11}}

(6.52)

or in terms of
\bar{p}
:

\displaystyle Cov[3SE]=

\displaystyle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})\bar{P}_{B11}}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{B11}}

(6.53)

6.7.3.2
Formula for
c_{k}

6.7.3.2.1  standard

Let us derive the formula for the
c_{k}
in case of 1 subevent.
The two particle correlation is given as :

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)(p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}}

(6.54)

The expansion of the denominator :

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{21}-S_{12}

The expansion of numerator :

\displaystyle N=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)(p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}\{p_{i}p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle p_{j}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}\}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}\{p_{i}^{2}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}-2p_{i}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{11}^{2}-2\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle P_{11}S_{11}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}S_{21}-P_{22}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}S_{12}+2\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle P_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{11}^{2}-P_{22}-2\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle(P_{11}S_{11}-P_{12})+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}(S_{21}-S_{12})

So the
c_{k}
is :

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{P_{11}^{2}-P_{22}-2\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle(P_{11}S_{11}-P_{12})+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle^{2}(S_{21}-S_{12})}{S_{21}-S_{12}}

(6.55)

or in terms of
\bar{p}
(We should simply use this form since we can use Eq. (
6.42
)):

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{\bar{P}_{11}^{2}-\bar{P}_{22}}{S_{21}-S_{12}}

(6.56)

6.7.3.2.2  Two-subevent

The two particle correlation is given as :

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A})(p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C})}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}}

(6.57)

In 2-subevent case the particle i and j belong to two different subevents - A and C and are never the same.

The expansion of the denominator :

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{A11}*S_{C11}

The expansion of numerator :

\displaystyle N=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}(p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A})(p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}\{p_{i}p_{j}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}p_{i}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}p_{j}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{A11}P_{C11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}P_{A11}S_{A11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}P_{C11}S_{C11}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}S_{A11}S_{C11}

So the
c_{k}
is :

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{P_{A11}P_{C11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}P_{A11}S_{A11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}P_{C11}S_{C11}+\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{A}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle_{C}S_{A11}S_{C11}}{S_{A11}*S_{C11}}

(6.58)

or in terms of
\bar{p}
:

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{\bar{P}_{A11}*\bar{P}_{C11}}{S_{A11}*S_{C11}}

(6.59)

6.7.3.3
Formula for
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2}
)

The variance of
v_{n}\{2\}^{2}
is given by :

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})=\langle\!\langle 4\rangle\!\rangle-\langle\!\langle 2\rangle\!\rangle^{2}

(6.60)

where
\langle 2\rangle
is the 2-particle correlation and
\langle 4\rangle
is the 4-particle correlation in an event.

6.7.3.3.1  standard

Two-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 2\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}}

(6.61)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}}

(6.62)

\displaystyle=\frac{|Q_{11}|^{2}-S_{12}}{S_{21}-S_{12}}

(6.63)

Four-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}}

(6.64)

The denominator can be expanded as :

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}-6\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}w_{h}-4\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}-3\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{4}

\displaystyle=S_{41}-6(S_{12}S_{11}S_{11}-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}-S_{14})-4(S_{13}S_{11}-S_{14})-3(S_{22}-S_{14})-S_{14}

\displaystyle=S_{41}-4S_{13}S_{11}-3S_{22}-6S_{21}S_{12}+12S_{14}+12\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}+6\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{41}-4S_{13}S_{11}-3S_{22}-6S_{21}S_{12}+12S_{14}+12(S_{13}S_{11}-S_{14})+6(S_{22}-S_{14})

\displaystyle=S_{41}+8S_{13}S_{11}+3S_{22}-6S_{21}S_{12}-6S_{14}

The numerator can be expanded as :

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}-4\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(\phi_{j}-\phi_{h})}

\displaystyle-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(2\phi_{i}-\phi_{j}-\phi_{h})}-4\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}e^{i2n(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}-\sum_{i}w_{i}^{4}

\displaystyle=|Q_{11}|^{4}-S_{14}

\displaystyle-4(S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}-S_{14})

\displaystyle-2(Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*}-2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}e^{i2n(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}-S_{14})

\displaystyle-4(Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*}-S_{14})-(|Q_{22}|^{2}-S_{14})-2(S_{22}-S_{14})

\displaystyle=|Q_{11}|^{4}-|Q_{22}|^{2}-2Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*}-4Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*}-4S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}-2S_{22}+12S_{14}

\displaystyle+12\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{3}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}+2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}e^{i2n(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}+4\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=|Q_{11}|^{4}-|Q_{22}|^{2}-2Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*}-4Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*}-4S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}-2S_{22}+12S_{14}

\displaystyle+12(Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*}-S_{14})+2(|Q_{22}|^{2}-S_{14})+4(S_{22}-S_{14})

\displaystyle=|Q_{11}|^{4}+|Q_{22}|^{2}-2Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*}+8Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*}-4S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}+2S_{22}-6S_{14}

So the final form of
\langle 4\rangle
is :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{|Q_{11}|^{4}+|Q_{22}|^{2}-2\Re(Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*})+8\Re(Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*})-4S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}+2S_{22}-6S_{14}}{S_{41}+8S_{13}S_{11}+3S_{22}-6S_{21}S_{12}-6S_{14}}

(6.65)

6.7.3.3.2  Two-subevent

Two-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 2\rangle_{a|c}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}e^{in(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})}}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}w_{i}w_{j}}

(6.66)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(Q_{A11}Q_{C11}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}}

(6.67)

Particle i is from subevent A and particle j is from subevent C.
Four-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle_{aa|cc}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}}

(6.68)

Particle i and j are from subevent A and particle h and l are from subevent C.
The denominator can be expanded as :

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}-\sum_{i}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{h}w_{l}-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}^{2}-\sum_{i}\sum_{h}w_{i}^{2}w_{h}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{A21}S_{C21}-(S_{A12}S_{C21}-S_{A12}S_{C12})-(S_{A21}S_{C12}-S_{A12}S_{C12})-S_{A12}S_{C12}

\displaystyle=S_{A21}S_{C21}-S_{A12}S_{C21}-S_{A21}S_{C12}+S_{A12}S_{C12}

\displaystyle=(S_{A21}-S_{A12})(S_{C21}-S_{C12})

The numerator can be expanded as :

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}w_{l}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\sum_{h}\sum_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}^{2}w_{j}w_{h}e^{in(2\phi_{i}-\phi_{h}-\phi_{l})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{\prime}\sum_{h}w_{i}w_{j}w_{h}^{2}e^{in(\phi_{i}+\phi_{j}-2\phi_{h})}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\sum_{h}w_{i}^{2}w_{h}^{2}e^{in2(\phi_{i}-\phi_{h})}

\displaystyle=|Q_{A11}Q_{C11}^{*}|^{2}-(Q_{A22}Q_{C11}^{*}Q_{C11}^{*}-Q_{A22}Q_{C22}^{*})

\displaystyle-(Q_{A11}Q_{A11}Q_{C22}^{*}-Q_{A22}Q_{C22}^{*})-Q_{A22}Q_{C22}^{*}

\displaystyle=|Q_{A11}Q_{C11}^{*}|^{2}-Q_{A22}Q_{C11}^{*}Q_{C11}^{*}-Q_{A11}Q_{A11}Q_{C22}^{*}+Q_{A22}Q_{C22}^{*}

\displaystyle=(Q_{A11}^{2}-Q_{A22})(Q_{C11}^{2}-Q_{C22})^{*}

So the final form of
\langle 4\rangle_{aa|cc}
is :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle_{aa|cc}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(Q_{A11}^{2}-Q_{A22})\Re(Q_{C11}^{*2}-Q_{C22}^{*})}{(S_{A21}-S_{A12})(S_{C21}-S_{C12})}

(6.69)

6.7.3.4
Formulae using
q
-vectors

Recall the definitions :

\displaystyle Q_{nk}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}e^{in\phi_{i}}

(6.70)

\displaystyle S_{mk}=

\displaystyle(\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k})^{m}

(6.71)

\displaystyle O_{nk}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}e^{in\phi_{i}}p_{i}

(6.72)

\displaystyle P_{mk}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k}p_{i}^{m}

(6.73)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{ev}}\sum_{evts}\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}p_{i}}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}

(6.74)

Lets define some normalised quantities :

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{nk}

\displaystyle=Q_{nk}/S_{1k}

(6.76)

\displaystyle\hat{o}_{nk}

\displaystyle=O_{nk}/S_{1k}

(6.77)

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{nk}

\displaystyle=P_{mk}/S_{1k}

(6.78)

\displaystyle\tau_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}^{k+1}}{(\sum_{i}w_{i})^{k+1}}

(6.79)

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{1,k+1}}{S_{k+1,1}}

(6.80)

1. FSE 2-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 2\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{|Q_{11}|^{2}-S_{12}}{S_{21}-S_{12}}

(6.81)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{11}|^{2}-S_{12}/S_{21}}{1-S_{12}/S_{21}}

(6.82)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{11}|^{2}-\tau_{1}}{1-\tau_{1}}

(6.83)

2. 2-SE 2-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 2\rangle_{a|c}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(Q_{A11}Q_{C11}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}}

(6.84)

\displaystyle=\Re(\hat{q}_{A11}\hat{q}_{C11}^{*})

(6.85)

3. FSE 4-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{|Q_{11}|^{4}+|Q_{22}|^{2}-2\Re(Q_{22}Q_{11}^{*}Q_{11}^{*})+8\Re(Q_{13}Q_{11}^{*})-4S_{12}|Q_{11}|^{2}+2S_{22}-6S_{14}}{S_{41}+8S_{13}S_{11}+3S_{22}-6S_{21}S_{12}-6S_{14}}

(6.86)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{11}|^{4}+\tau_{1}^{2}|\hat{q}_{22}|^{2}-2\tau_{1}\hat{q}_{22}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}+8\tau_{2}\hat{q}_{13}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}-4\tau_{1}|\hat{q}_{11}|^{2}+2\tau_{1}^{2}-6\tau_{3}}{1+8\tau_{2}+3\tau_{1}^{2}-6\tau_{1}-6\tau_{3}}

(6.87)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{11}|^{4}+\tau_{1}^{2}(2+|\hat{q}_{22}|^{2})-2\tau_{1}(\hat{q}_{22}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}+2|\hat{q}_{11}|^{2})+8\tau_{2}\hat{q}_{13}\hat{q}_{11}^{*}-6\tau_{3}}{1-6\tau_{1}+8\tau_{2}+3\tau_{1}^{2}-6\tau_{3}}

(6.88)

4. 2-SE 4-particle correlation :

\displaystyle\langle 4\rangle_{aa|cc}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(Q_{A11}^{2}-Q_{A22})\Re(Q_{C11}^{*2}-Q_{C22}^{*})}{(S_{A21}-S_{A12})(S_{C21}-S_{C12})}

(6.89)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(\hat{q}_{A11}^{2}-\tau_{A1}\hat{q}_{A22})\Re(\hat{q}_{C11}^{*2}-\tau_{C1}\hat{q}_{C22}^{*})}{(1-\tau_{A1})(1-\tau_{C1})}

(6.90)

5. 3-SE Covariance :

\displaystyle Cov[3SE]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{B11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{B11}}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{B11}}

(6.91)

\displaystyle=\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})\hat{p}_{B11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})

(6.92)

\displaystyle=\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})(\hat{p}_{B11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)

(6.93)

6. FSE Covariance :

\displaystyle Cov[FSE]

\displaystyle=\frac{|Q_{n1}|^{2}P_{11}-S_{12}P_{11}-2\Re(O_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2P_{13}}{S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}}

(6.94)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{|Q_{n1}|^{2}S_{11}-S_{12}S_{11}-2\Re(Q_{n2}Q_{n1}^{*})+2S_{13}}{S_{31}-3S_{12}S_{11}+2S_{13}}

(6.95)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{n1}|^{2}\hat{p}_{11}-\tau_{1}\hat{p}_{11}-2\tau_{1}\Re(\hat{o}_{n2}\hat{q}_{n1}^{*})+2\tau_{2}\hat{p}_{13}}{1-3\tau_{1}+2\tau_{2}}

(6.96)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{|\hat{q}_{n1}|^{2}-\tau_{1}-2\tau_{1}\Re(\hat{q}_{n2}\hat{q}_{n1}^{*})+2\tau_{2}}{1-3\tau_{1}+2\tau_{2}}

(6.97)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\hat{q}_{n1}|^{2}(\hat{p}_{11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\tau_{1}(\hat{p}_{11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-2\tau_{1}(\Re(\hat{o}_{n2}\hat{q}_{n1}^{*})-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(\hat{q}_{n2}\hat{q}_{n1}^{*}))+2\tau_{2}(\hat{p}_{13}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)}{1-3\tau_{1}+2\tau_{2}}

(6.98)

7. 2-SE Covariance :

\displaystyle Cov[2SE]=

\displaystyle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})P_{11}-\Re(O_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}O_{Cn2}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}}

(6.99)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn1}^{*})S_{11}-\Re(Q_{An2}Q_{Cn1}^{*})-\Re(Q_{An1}Q_{Cn2}^{*})}{S_{A11}S_{C11}S_{11}-S_{A12}S_{C11}-S_{A11}S_{C12}}

(6.100)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})\hat{p}_{11}-\tau_{A1}(S_{A11}/S_{11})\Re(\hat{o}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{C1}(S_{C11}/S_{11})\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{o}_{Cn2}^{*})}{1-\tau_{A1}(S_{A11}/S_{11})-\tau_{C1}(S_{C11}/S_{11})}

(6.101)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{A1}(S_{A11}/S_{11})\Re(\hat{q}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{C1}(S_{C11}/S_{11})\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn2}^{*})}{1-\tau_{A1}(S_{A11}/S_{11})-\tau_{C1}(S_{C11}/S_{11})}

(6.102)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})\hat{p}_{11}-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}\Re(\hat{o}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{o}_{Cn2}^{*})}{1-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}}

(6.103)

\displaystyle-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\frac{\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}\Re(\hat{q}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn2}^{*})}{1-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}}

(6.104)

\displaystyle=\{\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})(\hat{p}_{11}-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}(\Re(\hat{o}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*})-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(\hat{q}_{An2}\hat{q}_{Cn1}^{*}))

(6.105)

\displaystyle-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}(\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{o}_{Cn2}^{*})-\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle\Re(\hat{q}_{An1}\hat{q}_{Cn2}^{*}))\}/\{1-\tau_{A1}\alpha_{A}-\tau_{C1}\alpha_{C}\}

(6.106)

where
\alpha_{A}=S_{A11}/S_{11}
and
\alpha_{C}=S_{C11}/S_{11}
.

6.8
Comparison between different subevent methods for
v_{n}-p_{\text{T}}
Correlation (
\rho(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
)

6.8.1
Covariance comparison

The covariance
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])=\langle\langle v_{n}^{2}\,\delta p_{\text{T}}\rangle\rangle
as a function of
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
is shown in Figures
6.11
and
6.12
. The standard method exhibits elevated values in peripheral bins, indicating nonflow contamination. The two- and three-subevent methods converge at higher multiplicities; for
n=3,4
they agree within errors, while some deviation persists for
n=2
. Averaging the two- and three-subevent results (“2+3‐SE”) improves precision, and this average is used for
\mathrm{cov}(v_{3,4}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
. However, for
\mathrm{cov}(v_{2}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, the three‐subevent result alone is adopted.

Figure 6.11
:
Comparison of
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
using the standard, two-subevent and three-subevent methods in Pb+Pb for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

Figure 6.12
:
Comparison of
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
using the standard, two-subevent and three-subevent methods in Xe+Xe for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

6.8.2
Variance comparison

Figures
6.13
and
6.14
compare the variance
\mathrm{Var}\bigl((v_{n}\{2\})^{2}\bigr)_{\mathrm{dyn}}=c_{n}\{4\}-[c_{n}\{2\}]^{2}
evaluated using standard, two‐subevent, and hybrid methods [Eq. (
5.31
)]. The standard approach overestimates the variance in peripheral events. The hybrid prescription, which retains nonflow suppression while improving statistical precision, is chosen for
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
in the final
\rho_{n}
.

Figure 6.13
:
Comparison of
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
using the standard and two-subevent methods as well as method based on Eq.
5.31
in Pb+Pb for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

Figure 6.14
:
Comparison of
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
using the standard and two-subevent methods as well as method based on Eq.
5.31
in Xe+Xe for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

6.8.3
Pearson coefficient

Finally, three combinations for the Pearson coefficient
\rho_{n}
are tested:
3‐SE
(
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
from three‐subevent,
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
from two‐subevent),
2+3‐SE
(
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
averaged two‐ and three‐subevent,
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
two‐subevent), and
2+3‐SE‐Mod
(same
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, hybrid
\mathrm{var}(v_{n}^{2})
). These are shown in Figures
6.15
and
6.16
for
n=2,3,4
. All methods agree for
n=3,4
, with “2+3‐SE‐Mod” offering the best precision, and thus is adopted for the final
\rho_{n}
results.

Figure 6.15
:
Comparison of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
using different
\eta
-subevents in Pb+Pb collisions for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

Figure 6.16
:
Comparison of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
using different
\eta
-subevents in Xe+Xe collisions for
n=2
, 3 and 4. The result is obtained for the
|\eta|<2.5
. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties.

Some of the differences between the different subevent methods were previously observed in other analysis. Figure
6.17
is taken from the ATLAS publication on higher order cumulants in Pb+Pb
[
184
]
. It shows the comparison of the normalized four-particle cumulant
nc_{2}\{4\}
from standard and three-subevent methods in different
p_{\text{T}}
-ranges in Pb+Pb collisions. Significant differences were observed between the two methods over a wide range in centrality. These differences in
nc_{2}\{4\}
and also similar kind of differences in ATLAS publication of mixed-harmonic symmetric cumulants
[
196
]
were attributed to longitudinal flow decorrelations. Following the same logic, the differences in the observables we observe in this analysis between different methods based on subevents can also be attributed to flow decorrelations.

Figure 6.17
:
The
nc_{2}\{4\}
values calculated for charged particles in several
p_{\text{T}}
-ranges with the standard method (left) and three-subevent method (right). The error bars and shaded boxes represent the statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.

Due to applied changes in
\eta
-ranges in subevents A, B and C, not only the track multiplicity changed significantly but also changed the gap widths between sub-events A and C as well as the gap between sub-events A
+
C and B. This variation in
\eta
-gaps allowed the study of impact of “non-flow” correlations on the obsevables. Figure
6.18
shows the comparison of the observables -
\sqrt{c_{k}}/\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle
,
\mathrm{Var}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
,
\mathrm{cov}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
and
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
in Pb+Pb collisions for different
\eta
-gap selections between A, B and C. The observables were seen to depend very weakly on the
\eta
-gap choices.

Figure 6.18
:
\sqrt{c_{k}}/\langle[p_{\text{T}}]\rangle
,
\mathrm{Var}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
,
\mathrm{cov}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
and
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for different
\eta-gaps
selections between A, B and C in Pb+Pb collisions.

The primary systematic uncertainty in
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
stems from
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, with partial cancellation from tracking efficiency and track selection cuts. For
\mathrm{cov}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, systematics are
\lesssim 5\%
across all centralities. For
\mathrm{cov}(v_{3}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
and
\mathrm{cov}(v_{4}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, systematics are
\lesssim 5\%
for central/mid-central events; relative uncertainties are not quoted for peripheral collisions due to large statistical errors or sign changes, but full systematic uncertainties will be shown. Pileup contamination contributes
<0.5\%
systematic uncertainty across all centralities. Flattening has minimal systematic impact for all observables and centralities. For
n=2
, track quality variation and efficiency correlation dominate systematics for all observables. For
n=3,4
, efficiency variations contribute comparably or more to systematic uncertainty. Centrality percentile uncertainty is most significant in peripheral regions.

6.11
Statistical Error Estimation using Poisson Bootstrap Method

Estimating statistical errors for multi-particle cumulants and correlation observables, such as
\left\langle p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle
cumulants,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, and
\rho(v_{n}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
components, presents challenges for standard error propagation. This is because standard methods can lead to an overestimation of the statistical uncertainty, particularly for higher-order correlations and combinations involving statistically correlated quantities (e.g.,
\left\langle\mathrm{corr}_{n}\{4\}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathrm{corr}_{n}\{2\}\right\rangle^{2}
in
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
).

Therefore, robust resampling techniques like bootstrapping are employed for proper error estimation in this dissertation. Specifically, a **Poisson bootstrap** method is used to estimate the statistical errors for all observables. The choice of Poisson bootstrap is motivated by its computational efficiency compared to standard resampling; instead of randomly selecting events, it applies a Poisson weight (drawn from a distribution with a mean of one) to the observable calculated for each event. This method is mathematically justified when the number of events in a given event class is sufficiently large, such that the multinomial probability of event observables can be approximated by a Poisson probability.

Compared to straightforward analytical error calculations, the bootstrap errors are generally smaller, especially for higher-order correlations. For this analysis, Poisson bootstrap with 80 iterations for the Xe+Xe dataset and 60 iterations for the Pb+Pb dataset was performed to ensure reliable statistical error estimation for all observables presented.

6.12
Systematic Uncertainties

This section discusses the sources for systematic uncertainties and cross-checks.
The systematic uncertainties in these analyses arise from several common sources, which are detailed below:

1.

Track Quality Selection (HILOOSE vs HITIGHT):
The selection of tracks based on cuts applied to parameters such as
d_{0}
,
z_{0}\sin\theta
, pixel hits, and SCT hits leads to two primary categories: HILOOSE (looser cuts) and HITIGHT (tighter cuts). The choice between these selections affects the tracking efficiency and the balance between real and fake tracks. In some analyses, the average of measurements obtained with HILOOSE and HITIGHT tracks is taken as the default value to symmetrize the systematic variation. The variation due to track quality is often treated as a one-sided uncertainty, and in such cases, half of the difference between the HILOOSE and HITIGHT results is added symmetrically to the total systematic uncertainty.

2.

Tracking Efficiency Uncertainty:
The efficiency of track reconstruction depends on the number of charged particles (
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
), transverse momentum (
p_{T}
), and pseudorapidity (
\eta
). Uncertainties in the tracking efficiency, typically ranging from 1% to 4%, stem from discrepancies in the detector material budget between the experimental data and the simulations (GEANT). These uncertainties are often evaluated by varying the efficiency according to a specific functional form, such as

\epsilon_{\pm}(p_{\text{T}})\equiv\epsilon(p_{\text{T}})\pm 0.04\frac{\epsilon(p_{\text{T}})-\epsilon(p_{\text{T}}^{\mathrm{high}}=5\mathrm{GeV})}{\epsilon(p_{\text{T}}^{\mathrm{low}}=0.5\mathrm{GeV})-\epsilon(p_{\text{T}}^{\mathrm{high}}=5\mathrm{GeV})}.

(6.107)

3.

Centrality Definition Uncertainty (Lower vs Higher FCal-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
Cuts):
The determination of centrality intervals is subject to uncertainties related to the min-bias trigger efficiency. This can lead to a
\pm 1\%
uncertainty in the nominal centrality range. The impact of this uncertainty is assessed by re-evaluating observables with slightly shifted centrality ranges (e.g., for a nominal range of 0-85.5%, variations to 0-84.5% and 0-86.5% are considered). This source of uncertainty is relevant when results are presented as a function of centrality percentiles.

4.

\phi
-Flattening Procedure:
Detector effects can cause non-uniformities in the azimuthal angle (
\phi
) distribution of tracks. A
\phi
-flattening procedure, involving track weights, is applied to correct for these effects. The systematic uncertainty associated with this correction is estimated by repeating the analysis without applying the
\phi
-flattening.

5.

Pileup Contamination:
To estimate the systematic effect from pileup contamination, the significance cut applied on the ZDC-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
correlation plot is varied from 6-
\sigma
to 7-
\sigma
. The resulting systematic effect on the observables is found to be less than
0.5\%
for all observables and all centralities.

The relative systematic uncertainty for an individual source is generally calculated as

\delta_{\mathrm{sys}}=\frac{{O}_{\mathrm{check}}-O_{\mathrm{default}}}{O_{\mathrm{default}}},

(6.108)

where
O
is the observable. For observables that can take values close to zero, the absolute uncertainty,

\delta_{\mathrm{sys,Abs}}={O}_{\mathrm{check}}-O_{\mathrm{default}}

(6.109)

6.12.1
For
N_{\text{ch}}

Using efficiency corrected
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
,
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch}}
as event class introduces systematic uncertainties arising from the efficiency and Fake rates used to calculate it. A described in the previous section, the efficiency and Fake rates have their own associated uncertainties, which, lead to an uncertainty on the calculated
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch}}
.

In this analysis,
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
is used as the default choice. The difference between
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch}}
calculated for different systematic variations and
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
are shown in left panel of fig.
6.25
as a function of
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
. The systematic contribution from track selection criteria (HITight) is symmetrized to account for systematics from a more relaxed tack selection criteria than HILoose. The maximum contribution to the total systematics on
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
arises from the
\pm 4\%
efficiency variation. The magnitude of total systematics for a given value of
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
is obtained from taking a square-root of the quadrature-sum of different contributions for a given value of
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
. In most central collisions, the total systematic uncertainty on the
\textit{N}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
is around 3%.

Figure 6.25
:
(Left) Different Systematic contributions for
N_{\text{ch}}
as a function of the default choice of
N_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
, (Right) Different Systematic contributions for
N_{\text{ch}}/N^{5\%}_{ch}
as a function of the default choice of
N_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}/N^{5\%}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
, in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=
5.02 TeV. The red line shows the total systematics obtained by taking a square-root of the quadrature-sum of different systematic contributions.

Similarly, this analysis also uses
N_{\text{ch}}/N^{5\%}_{ch}
as event class to plot certain results, which has its own associated systematic uncertainty. The different systematic contributions for
N_{\text{ch}}/N^{5\%}_{ch}
are shown in the right panel of Fig.
6.25
. The systematic variations are smoothed and conservative estimates are shown for each contribution. After normalization by
N^{5\%}_{ch}
, the largest systematic contribution arises from track quality variation from HILoose to HITight. For largest value of
N_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}/N^{5\%}_{\mathrm{ch,Loose}}
, the total systematic uncertainty is around 1.5%.

6.12.2
For
[p_{\text{T}}]
Cumulants

The systematic uncertainties for the cumulants
k_{n}
(
n=2,3
) and their normalized counterparts (
nk_{2}
,
\gamma
,
\Gamma
) are evaluated for particles within
|\eta|<2.5
and
0.5<p_{T}<5.0
GeV for Pb+Pb collisions and
0.3<p_{T}<5.0
GeV for Xe+Xe collisions. Similar to the
v_{0}(p_{T})
analysis, the track quality selection, tracking efficiency, and centrality definition contribute to the systematic uncertainties. The contribution from the track quality variation (HILOOSE vs HITIGHT) is typically symmetrized by dividing the difference by two and adding it symmetrically to the total systematic uncertainty. For higher-order measurements, statistical fluctuations in the systematic contributions are smoothed out by averaging neighboring points. The HIJING closure check, performed for Pb+Pb collisions, provides an estimate of the non-closure, which is then added in quadrature to the total systematic uncertainty for both Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe systems. The systematic effect from pileup contamination is estimated to be less than
0.5\%
for these observables across all centralities.

There are several observables in the analysis, each with several methods and
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
selections. Instead of showing the systematic checks for all of them, we focus on the three most important physics quantities:
k_{n}
for
n=2
, 3 and their normalized counterparts, Normalized variance (
nk_{2}
), Normalized skewness (
\gamma
) and intensive skewness (
\Gamma
) using the standard method for particles within
|\eta|<
2.5 and 0.5
<p_{\text{T}}
¡5.0 GeV for Pb+Pb collisions and 0.3
<p_{\text{T}}
¡5.0 GeV for Xe+Xe collisions. In the following, we summarize them separately. The systematics for Pb+Pb collisions are shown in Section
6.12.2.1
and for Xe+Xe collisions are shown in Section
6.12.2.2
.

Please note that all sources of systematics have an upward and downward variation about the default measurement whereas, the track quality variation is one-sided (i.e varied between HILOOSE and HITIGHT). Therefore the contribution from track quality variation is divided by 2 and is added symmetrically between the upper and lower limits of the total systematic variations in both systems (denoted as ‘TightSym’ on the bottom panel of the systematics plots). In addition, there are statistical jumps going from one bin to the next for the systematic contributions for higher-order measurements. In this case, the points are averaged taking the neighborhood points into account to smooth out the jumps.

6.12.2.1
In Pb+Pb Collisions

Fig.
6.26
shows the comparison of different systematic checks with the default case for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
and
k_{3}
in Pb+Pb collisions measured with the standard method. The bottom panels show relative difference w.r.t default along with the lower and upper bounds shown in lines and the ratio of statistical uncertainty of the default to its mean value shown as a shaded region. The systematic variation is within
10\%
and is dominated by track selection and centrality definition. The systematic uncertainties are larger than statistical uncertainties for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
and
k_{3}
.

Figure 6.26
:
Comparison between different systematic variations and default measurement for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
(Left panel),
k_{2}
(Central panel) and
k_{3}
(Right panel) for 0.5-5 GeV particles in Xe+Xe collisions. The bottom panels show relative systematic uncertainties arising from systematic variations w.r.t default for respective observables on top panels. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties. The shaded area represents the ratio of the statistical uncertainty. Solid red-colored lines on the bottom panels reflect combined systematic uncertainty for the observables.

Fig.
6.27
shows the comparison of different systematic checks with the default case for normalized
\left\langle p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle
-fluctuations in Pb+Pb (From left to right:
nk_{2}
,
\gamma
and
\Gamma
). The bottom panels show relative difference w.r.t default along with the lower and upper bounds shown in lines and the ratio of statistical uncertainty of the default to its mean value shown as a shaded region. The systematic uncertainty partially cancels between the numerator and denominator. Therefore the final systematic uncertainty has weaker centrality dependence. Normalization also cancels systematics for the observables. The systematic uncertainty for normalized observables in Pb+Pb collisions is within 4%.

Figure 6.27
:
Comparison between different systematic variations and default measurement for
nk_{2}
(Left panel),
\gamma
(Central panel) and
\Gamma
(Right panel) for 0.5-5 GeV particles in Pb+Pb collisions. The bottom panels show relative systematic uncertainties arising from systematic variations w.r.t default for respective observables on top panels. The error bars represent statistical uncertainties. The shaded area represents the ratio of the statistical uncertainty. Solid red-colored lines on the bottom panels reflect combined systematic uncertainty for the observables.

To calculate the total systematics, the points that are above 0 in these figures are added in quadrature to find the upper bound. Similarly, all the relative error points that are below 0 are added in quadrature to find the lower bound.

For
n=2
, the dominant source of systematics arises from tracking quality variation and efficiency correction for all observables. For
n=3
, the systematic uncertainty arising from efficiency variations becomes comparable to those arising from track quality variation. Uncertainty on the centrality definition has the largest impact in the most peripheral region. Only in the peripheral region, for some observables, the systematics becomes significant, but this is mainly due to large statistical uncertainties in the data. For all observables, in general, the total systematic error is smaller than or comparable to the statistical error.

We carry out a closure check using the HIJING model as a useful validation of the analysis method.

In the HIJING model, the
\left\langle p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle
fluctuations arise purely from an independent superposition scenario. Therefore, the model predictions for
\left\langle p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle
fluctuations are not expected to provide good estimates of these quantities. However, a study using HIJING events provides an estimate of the effect of short-range correlations, like jets or resonance decays, on the measured quantities
[
107
]
.

We compare the efficiency-corrected observables measured from reconstructed tracks with those measured with only truth level primary particles. A perfect closure would imply that measurements from both the aforementioned cases would agree exactly with each other. Fig.
6.28
shows the comparisons for measurements carried out using reconstructed tracks (corrected for efficiency and fake tracks) and primary particles at the truth level from the Pb+Pb MC sample as a function of centrality. Owing to small event statistics (
\sim
1 Million) in the HIJING MC sample, the observables are binned in wide bins of centrality. The comparisons are carried out for particles falling within the acceptance of 0.5
<p_{T}<
5.0 GeV and
|\eta|
¡ 2.5. The measurements with generated particles are observed to be in good agreement with the observables measured with reconstructed tracks after using track weights.

Figure 6.28
:
Comparison between observables measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles using HIJING MC sample for Pb+Pb collisions at 5 TeV. Top row shows
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
, and
k_{3}
, Bottom row shows
nk_{2}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
. The observables are plotted against
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
based centrality for particles with 0.5
<p_{T}<
5.0 GeV and
|\eta|<
2.5. Error bars denote statistical uncertainties.

The level of agreement shown by the observables measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles is not perfect. Therefore, to quantify the level of closure for the observables, Fig.
6.29
shows the ratio of observables measured with reconstructed tracks (corrected for efficiency and fake tracks) and primary particles as a function of centrality. The ratio for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
, and
nk_{2}
show closure within 0.1%, 0.8%, and 0.3% on average, respectively, over the entire range of centrality. The statistical fluctuations in the ratio for
k_{3}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
are observed to be relatively large owing to limited event statistics. Nevertheless, on average,
k_{3}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
show closure within an averaged value of 2.6%, 2.1% and 2.1% respectively over the entire centrality range. However, for the normalized observables, the error on the extracted average of the ratios for closure (shown in green faded bands) is also large and overlaps with 1.

Figure 6.29
:
Ratios between observables measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles using HIJING MC sample for Pb+Pb collisions at 5 TeV. Top row shows ratios for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
, and
k_{3}
, Bottom row shows ratios for
nk_{2}
,
\gamma
, and
\Gamma
. The ratios are plotted against
N_{\text{ch}}^{\text{rec}}
based centrality for particles with 0.5
<p_{T}<
5.0 GeV and
|\eta|<
2.5. Error bars denote statistical uncertainties. The dotted horizontal line denotes the average of all the points, whereas the faded band denotes the error on the calculated average of the points.

To account for the level of non-closure in total systematics, we add a symmetric contribution to the total systematics in quadrature. Therefore, the quoted value of systematic contribution arising from non-closure in this study for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
stand at
\pm
0.05
%,
\pm
0.4
%,
\pm
1.3
% respectively. For the normalized observables,
nk_{2}
,
\gamma
and
\Gamma
, the systematic contribution from non-closure are
\pm
0.15
%,
\pm
1.05
%,
\pm
1.05
% respectively.

The closure check in this study is carried out for the Pb+Pb collisions production leveraging the relatively larger event statistics in the HIJING MC sample compared to Xe+Xe MC sample. The systematic uncertainties extracted for the observables using the Pb+Pb HIJING sample are also used for observables measured in Xe+Xe collisions.

6.12.2.2
In Xe+Xe Collisions

The systematics in Xe+Xe collisions are carried out in a very similar manner as is done for Pb+Pb collisions in Section
6.12.2.1
.

Fig.
6.30
shows the comparison of different systematic checks with the default case of
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
correlations in Xe+Xe. The bottom panels show relative difference w.r.t. default along with the lower and upper bounds shown in lines and the ratio of statistical uncertainty of the default to its mean value shown as a shaded region. The systematics are dominated by uncertainty in the track efficiency and track selection in the central and mid-central region. The uncertainty due to centrality is important in the peripheral region. The systematic uncertainty is generally larger than the statistical uncertainties for mean, variance, and skewness.

The total systematic error is comparable to or smaller than the statistical uncertainties for all centralities.

6.12.3
For
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})

For the measurements of
\left\langle\delta N(p_{T})\delta p_{T}\right\rangle
,
v_{0}(p_{\mathrm{T}})
, and
v_{0}(p_{\mathrm{T}})/v_{0}
using tracks within
|\eta|<2.5
and
0.5<p_{T}<5.0
GeV in Pb+Pb collisions, the dominant systematic uncertainties arise from the track quality selection and tracking efficiency. The centrality definition uncertainty also plays a role, particularly when results are presented as a function of centrality. The HIJING closure check provides an additional estimate of potential systematic biases related to short-range correlations and detector effects. The level of non-closure observed in HIJING is accounted for by adding a symmetric contribution to the total systematic uncertainty in quadrature. For instance, for
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
,
k_{2}
, and
k_{3}
, the systematic contributions from non-closure are
\pm 0.05\%
,
\pm 0.4\%
, and
\pm 1.3\%
, respectively. The systematic effect from pileup contamination is estimated to be less than
0.5\%
for these observables across all centralities.

Figure
6.32
compares various systematic checks with the default measurement for
\left\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
as functions of
p_{\text{T}}
in Pb+Pb collisions for mid-central 30-40% centrality. The bottom panels illustrate the absolute differences with respect to the default case, with the lower and upper bounds represented as lines, and the the statistical uncertainty of the default measurement as a shaded region. Additionally, it shows the relative systematic variations compared to the default measurement for
v_{0}
as a function of centrality.

To calculate the total systematics, the points that are above 0 in these figures are added in quadrature to find the upper bound.
For
v_{0}
, the primary source of systematic uncertainty arises from tracking quality variations, which are most significant in central collisions, and from centrality selection variations, which dominate in peripheral collisions. For
\left\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
, the observables change sign around
p_{\text{T}}\approx\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
. Since
\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
is slightly larger for tight tracks compared to loose tracks, the systematic uncertainty related to tracking quality also changes sign near
p_{\text{T}}=\langle[p_{T}]\rangle
. In
\left\langle\delta n(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
, tracking quality variation is the dominant source of systematic uncertainty, followed by efficiency corrections and centrality selection variations. For
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, the presence of a denominator suppresses systematic uncertainties from track quality criteria, making efficiency corrections dominant in the low
p_{\text{T}}
region and centrality corrections dominant in the high
p_{\text{T}}
region. Since
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
is centrality-independent, centrality variations contribute minimally to its systematic uncertainties. However, the inclusion of
v_{0}
in the denominator causes tracking quality criteria to dominate, with efficiency corrections being a close secondary source.

We also do one additional check to estimate the systematic effect from pileup contamination, we nominally vary the significance cut applied on ZDC-
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
correlation plot from 6
\sigma
to 7
\sigma
and estimate the systematic effect on the observables as a function of centrality. The effect is observed to be negligible for all the observables in this analysis.

Figure 6.32
:
Comparison of different absolute systematic variations with the default measurement for
\left\langle\delta N(p_{\text{T}})\delta[p_{\text{T}}]\right\rangle
,
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
as functions of
p_{\text{T}}
in Pb+Pb collisions for 30–40% centrality. Additionally, a comparison of relative systematic variations with the default measurement for
v_{0}
is shown as a function of centrality. The bottom panels depict the absolute uncertainties arising from these systematic variations with respect to the default measurement for the corresponding observables shown in the top panels. The solid red lines in the bottom panels denote the combined systematic uncertainty for the observables.

We carry out a closure check using the HIJING model as a useful validation of the analysis method. In the HIJING model, the
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations arise purely from an independent superposition scenario. Therefore, the model predictions for
[p_{\text{T}}]
fluctuations are not expected to provide good estimates of these quantities.

We compare the efficiency-corrected observables measured from reconstructed tracks with those measured with only truth level primary particles. A perfect closure would imply that measurements from both the aforementioned cases would agree exactly with each other. Figures.
6.33
and
6.34
show the comparisons of measurements carried out using reconstructed tracks (corrected for efficiency and Fake tracks) and primary particles at the truth level from the Pb+Pb MC sample as a function of centrality. Owing to small event statistics (
\approx
3 Million) in the HIJING MC sample, the observables are binned in wide bins of centrality for
v_{0}
in Fig.
6.33
and in wide bins of
p_{\text{T}}
for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
in Fig.
6.34
.

The measurements with generated particles are observed to be in good agreement with the observables measured with reconstructed tracks after using track weights.

Figure 6.33
:
Comparison between
v_{0}
measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles using HIJING MC sample for Pb+Pb collisions at 5 TeV.
The observables are plotted against
N_{\text{ch}}
based centrality for particles Error bars denote statistical uncertainties. Ratios between
v_{0}
measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles are also shown on the right. The dotted horizontal line denotes the average of all the points, whereas the faded band denotes the error on the calculated average of the points.

Figure 6.34
:
Comparison between
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles using HIJING MC sample for Pb+Pb collisions at 5 TeV. The observables are plotted against
p_{\text{T}}
for
p_{T}
reference of 0.5-5.0 GeV and
|\eta|<
2.5 and for 0-80% centrality. Error bars denote statistical uncertainties calculated with bootstrap method (Sec.
6.11
).

However, the level of agreement shown by the observables measured with reconstructed tracks and primary particles is not perfect. To account for the level of non-closure in total systematics, we add a symmetric contribution to the total systematics in quadrature. Therefore, the quoted value of systematic contribution arising from non-closure for
v_{0}
stands at
\pm
1%.

Figure
6.34
presents the closure tests for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
and
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\mathrm{NN}}}=5.02
TeV. The results are consistent between measurements done with truth particles and reconstructed tracks across the entire
p_{\text{T}}
range. However, minor discrepancies are observed, reflected by the ratios of measurements between truth particles and reconstructed tracks, as shown in the bottom panels. Notably, the statistical uncertainties shown in Fig.
6.34
are derived using the Poisson bootstrap method, indicating that the observed variations in
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
between the two measurement approaches are well within expected statistical fluctuations. On an average, for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})
, the non-closure is about
\pm 2\%
and about
\pm 1.5\%
for
v_{0}(p_{\text{T}})/v_{0}
. These conservative estimates of non-closure are added to the total systematic uncertainty in quadrature.

Similarly, all the points that are below 0 are added in quadrature to find the lower bound.

6.12.4
For
v_{n}
-
[p_{\text{T}}]
Correlation

The systematic uncertainties for the covariance
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, dynamic variance
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
, correlation coefficient
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for
n=2,3,4
, and the two-particle
p_{T}
correlator
c_{k}=\left\langle\delta p_{\text{T}}\delta p_{\text{T}}\right\rangle
are also considered. The primary sources of systematic uncertainty include track quality selection, tracking efficiency, and centrality definition. Additionally, the
\phi
-flattening procedure introduces a systematic uncertainty, which is assessed by comparing results with and without this correction. The HIJING model is used as a cross-check to evaluate potential biases from short-range correlations and detector effects. The HIJING MC studies indicate negligible covariances between
v_{2}\{2\}^{2}
and
[p_{\text{T}}]
, and the dynamic variance of
v_{n}\{2\}^{2}
is also found to be small in the model. The
p_{T}
cumulants
c_{k}
show qualitative agreement between data and HIJING predictions. The systematic effect from pileup contamination is estimated to be less than
0.5\%
for these observables across all centralities.

In Xe+Xe Collisions

The systematics in Xe+Xe collisions are carried out in very similar manner. However due to the limited event statistics, the systematic uncertainty has significant statistical fluctuation, which require some smoothing as a function centrality. The relative uncertainty is calculated between the standard measurement and the varied checks defined by
(varied/nominal)-1
which is the figure of merit for the systematics checks.

11\%
over the entire centrality range except the peripheral centrality for
n=4
where contribution from track selection dominates. The systematics are dominated by uncertainty in the track efficiency and track selection in central and mid-central region. The uncertainty due to centrality is important in the peripheral region. The systematic uncertainty are generally larger than the statistical uncertainties for all harmonics.

6.12.4.3
Cross-checks

There are several cross-checks that were carried out, as discussed here.

•

Contamination from pileup events
Figure
6.43
shows the relative contribution from Pileup in each centrality interval for the measurement of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for
n=2
, 3 and 4 in Xe+Xe collisions. It can be observed that the difference arising from pileup contribution is very close to zero for
n=2
and 3. For
n=4
, this difference is close to zero for all centrality intervals except in most peripheral where the statistical uncertainty are very large.

Figure 6.43
:
Comparison of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
with and without pileup contributions removed for
n=2
,3 and 4 for
p_{\text{T}}
-range of 0.5-5 GeV. For
\rho(v_{4}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
, the effect of pileup is minimal in all centralities for all harmonics.

•

Residual Sine terms

For perfect detector operations, the imaginary part of the
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
and as a consequence that of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
should equal to zero. A ’Perfect’ flattening of the
q-
vectors as shown in Section
6.9.2
should remove any nonzero sine term. Therefore, a systematic check of the Im(
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
) gives an idea of the residual detector non-uniformities and robustness of the analysis method. Figure
6.44
shows the Imaginary component of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
in each centrality interval along with the measurement of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for
n=2
, 3 and 4. Figure
6.44
indeed shows that the Im(
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
) are consistent with the zero within statistical errors.

Figure 6.44
:
Residual Sine term in measurement of
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for
n=2
,3 and 4 for
p_{\text{T}}
-range of 0.5-5 GeV.The residual term is equal to zero within errors for
\rho(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
for all harmonics.

•

HIJING Closure check

By construction, in the HIJING model the generated flow harmonics are not related either to the geometry of the initial condition of heavy ion collisions or to the transverse momentum fluctuations. Therefore, the model predictions for
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
and
\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
are not expected to provide good estimates of these quantities even on qualitative basis. However, one can still study with the HIJING events the effect of non-flow correlations, like jets or resonance decays, on the measured quantities
[
107
]
. The single particle
v_{n}
has been imposed on the HIJING, but no genuine higher-order flow correlations or
v_{n}-[p_{\text{T}}]
correlations are expected.

It was found that much larger fluctuations of
v_{2}^{2}
harmonic are present in data than in HIJING MC for all
p_{\text{T}}
ranges of references particles. Similarly, comparing the covariances
\mathrm{cov}(v_{2}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])
in Figure
6.45
one can see that there are negligible covariances in the MC. Similarly, the magnitude of
\mathrm{Var}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2})_{\mathrm{dyn}}
observed in MC was also negligible. The
c_{k}
for MC showed a qualitative agreement between data and HIJING predictions although on quantitative level small differences were observed. Although HIJING does not properly describe the investigated quantities we think its still useful to be considered as a validation of the analysis method. In fact it points to the fact that the correlation observed in data does not arise from a trivial upward
p_{\text{T}}
fluctuation which would naively result in higher observed
v_{n}
value and both of these effects are present in MC yet the correlation is effectively absent. We did not carry out a HIJING closure check for Xe+Xe system, although it is expected the conclusion should be similar.

Figure 6.45
:

\mathrm{cov}(v_{n}\{2\}^{2},[p_{\text{T}}])

as a function of Nch in 5.02 TeV Pb+Pb collisions for different
p_{\text{T}}
thresholds.
